+      <div class="def">
+        <span class="tag">Definition</span>
+        <p><b>Radiation</b> is energy travelling from one place to another, either as an electromagnetic wave or as a stream of fast particles. It does not need a medium: light and X-rays cross a vacuum, and so do α and β particles.</p>

+          <tr><td>Radio, microwave, infrared, visible, ultraviolet, X-rays, γ rays</td><td>Electron beams, α particles, β particles, neutrons</td></tr>

         <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p>(3) is the definition: every EM wave carries energy from one place to another. (1) is false because EM waves and nuclear particles cross a vacuum; sunlight reaches us through empty space. (2) is false as a general statement: an EM wave transfers energy but no mass, and even a particle beam is described as radiation because of the energy it carries, not the mass.</p>

+      <div class="def">
+        <span class="tag">Definition</span>
+        <p><b>Ionizing radiation</b> carries enough energy per photon or per particle to remove an electron from an atom or molecule, turning it into an ion. Radiation that cannot do this is <b>non-ionizing</b>.</p>
       </div>
-      <table class="compact pair">
-        <thead><tr><th></th><th>ionizing</th><th>non-ionizing</th></tr></thead>

+          <tr><th>Energy per photon or particle</th><td>Enough to knock an electron out of an atom or molecule</td><td>Not enough; at most the electron is raised to a higher energy level and falls back</td></tr>
+          <tr><th>Examples</th><td>X-rays, γ rays, most ultraviolet; α and β particles</td><td>Radio, microwaves, infrared, visible light, the lowest-energy part of ultraviolet</td></tr>
+          <tr><th>Hazard</th><td>Ions and broken bonds inside cells damage tissue and DNA; cells may die or become cancerous</td><td>Heating at high intensity only; no ionization</td></tr>
         </tbody>
       </table>

-      <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="B">
+      <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="C">
         <h3>Concept check</h3>
-        <p>What does ionizing radiation do to an atom?</p>
+        <p>Which of the following statements about ionizing radiation is/are correct?</p>
+        <ol class="stem-list">
+          <li>Its energy is high enough to strike electrons out of atoms or molecules.</li>
+          <li>It is radiation in the form of ions.</li>
+          <li>X-rays are ionizing radiation.</li>
+        </ol>
         <div class="choices">
-          <button type="button" data-choice="A">It raises a bound electron to a higher shell, but the electron stays in the atom.</button>
-          <button type="button" data-choice="B">It knocks electrons out of atoms or molecules, turning them into ions.</button>
-          <button type="button" data-choice="C">It knocks protons out of the nucleus.</button>
-          <button type="button" data-choice="D">It has too little energy to knock electrons out.</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="A">(1) only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="B">(2) only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="C">(1) and (3) only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="D">(2) and (3) only</button>
         </div>
         <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p>(1) is the definition. (3) is true: X-ray photons carry far more energy than the few eV needed to free an electron. (2) is the common slip: "ionizing" describes what the radiation <em>does</em> to matter, not what it is made of. X-rays and γ rays carry no charge at all and still ionize; a beam of ions would be something else entirely.</p>
+        </div>

     <section class="idea" id="spectrum">
       <header>
-        <h2>Non-ionizing and ionizing EM waves</h2>

+      <figure class="fig">
+        <div class="visual play stage" id="spectrum-vis" data-scene="spectrum">
+          <canvas class="scene-canvas" width="960" height="280" aria-label="EM spectrum: ionizing barrier on the low-frequency side of UV"></canvas>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="nonion">non-ionizing</span>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="ion">ionizing</span>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="gamma">Gamma ray</span>
+        </div>
+        <figcaption><b>Fig. 25.5</b> The electromagnetic spectrum with frequency increasing to the right. The ionizing barrier sits inside the ultraviolet band, close to its low-frequency end.</figcaption>
+      </figure>
+      <table class="notes spectrum-bands">
         <colgroup>
           <col class="band-radio">
           <col class="band-micro">
@@ -140,45 +167,61 @@
           </tr>
         </tbody>
       </table>
-      <table class="compact pair">
-        <thead><tr><th>What you see</th><th>Therefore</th></tr></thead>
-        <tbody>
-          <tr><td>Higher frequency on the bar (to the right)</td><td>More energy per photon</td></tr>
-          <tr><td>Barrier far on the low-frequency side of UV</td><td>About one-tenth of UV is non-ionizing; most UV, X-rays and Gamma rays are ionizing</td></tr>
-        </tbody>
-      </table>
+      <ul class="points">
+        <li>Energy per photon rises with frequency (and falls with wavelength). Only photons above the ionization threshold can knock an electron out, so the ionizing part of the spectrum is the high-frequency, short-wavelength end.</li>
+        <li>Radio waves, microwaves, infrared and visible light are non-ionizing. The boundary lies inside the ultraviolet band; radiation of higher frequency than ultraviolet, that is X-rays and γ rays, is ionizing.</li>
+        <li>Scale to remember: visible light \( \lambda \sim 5 \times 10^{-7}\ \mathrm{m} \); X-rays \( \lambda \sim 10^{-11} \) to \( 10^{-8}\ \mathrm{m} \), \( f \sim 10^{18}\ \mathrm{Hz} \); γ rays are shorter still, \( f \sim 10^{20}\ \mathrm{Hz} \). Short wavelength goes with high photon energy, hence both ionizing and highly penetrating.</li>
+      </ul>
       <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="B">
         <h3>Concept check</h3>
-        <p>On the EM spectrum, where does ionizing radiation begin?</p>
+        <p>Which of the following properties are common to X-rays and to nuclear radiation (α, β, γ)?</p>
+        <ol class="stem-list">
+          <li>ionizing</li>
+          <li>emitted spontaneously</li>
+          <li>emitted from the nucleus</li>
+          <li>invisible</li>
+        </ol>
         <div class="choices">
-          <button type="button" data-choice="A">All ultraviolet is non-ionizing; only X-rays and Gamma rays ionize.</button>
-          <button type="button" data-choice="B">The barrier is far on the low-frequency side of UV: most of UV is ionizing, about one-tenth is not.</button>
-          <button type="button" data-choice="C">Infrared is already ionizing.</button>
-          <button type="button" data-choice="D">Visible light is ionizing.</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="A">(1) and (2) only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="B">(1) and (4) only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="C">(1), (3) and (4) only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="D">(1), (2), (3) and (4)</button>
         </div>
         <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p>Both are ionizing and both are invisible, so (1) and (4). (2) fails for X-rays: a tube emits only while its supply is on, so the emission is controlled, not spontaneous. (3) fails too: X-rays come from electrons decelerating in a metal target, outside any nucleus. These two differences (origin and spontaneity) are the whole distinction between an X-ray and a γ ray, which are otherwise the same kind of wave; see Section G.</p>
+        </div>
       </div>
     </section>

-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="gun">electron gun</span>

-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="xrays">X-rays</span>
-      </div>
-      <table class="compact pair">
-        <thead><tr><th>What you see</th><th>Therefore</th></tr></thead>
+      <figure class="fig">
+        <div class="visual play stage" id="tube-vis" data-autoplay data-scene="tube">
+          <canvas class="scene-canvas" width="960" height="420" aria-label="X-ray tube: electron gun, electrons hitting an angled heavy-metal target, X-rays leaving the target face"></canvas>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="gun">electron gun</span>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="electrons">electrons</span>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="target">heavy metal target</span>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="xrays">X-rays</span>
+        </div>
+        <figcaption><b>Fig. 25.6</b> An X-ray tube. Electrons from the gun are accelerated across the evacuated tube and strike the angled heavy-metal target; X-rays leave from the point of impact.</figcaption>
+      </figure>
+      <table class="notes">
+        <thead><tr><th>Part of the tube</th><th>What it does</th></tr></thead>
         <tbody>
-          <tr><td>Electrons hit the angled metal face; X-rays leave that impact in a wide fan</td><td>X-rays are EM waves made from the electrons' KE, not from the gun itself</td></tr>
-          <tr><td>X-rays: uncharged, speed c, blacken film</td><td>They are ionizing EM, not a beam of electrons</td></tr>
+          <tr><td>Heated filament (cathode)</td><td>Emits electrons when hot, so a current can flow across the tube.</td></tr>
+          <tr><td>Very high voltage (tens of kV) between cathode and target</td><td>Accelerates the electrons to very high speed. A higher voltage gives electrons more kinetic energy and so more penetrating X-rays.</td></tr>
+          <tr><td>Vacuum inside the glass</td><td>Electrons must not lose energy by colliding with air molecules on the way.</td></tr>
+          <tr><td>Heavy metal target (e.g. tungsten), angled</td><td>Electrons are stopped abruptly; part of their kinetic energy is emitted as X-rays, which leave through the side of the tube.</td></tr>
+          <tr><td>Cooling (oil, rotating target)</td><td>Only about 1% of the electrons' kinetic energy becomes X-rays; the rest heats the target.</td></tr>
         </tbody>
       </table>
+      <div class="def">
+        <span class="tag">Properties of X-rays</span>
+        <p>X-rays are electromagnetic waves of very short wavelength (about \( 10^{-10}\ \mathrm{m} \)). They carry no charge and no mass, travel at \( c \), are not deflected by electric or magnetic fields, pass through soft materials but are absorbed by dense ones, ionize atoms, blacken photographic film and cause some materials to fluoresce.</p>
+      </div>
       <div class="check" data-check="tf">
         <h3>Concept check</h3>
         <div class="tf-item" data-answer="false">
@@ -188,127 +231,204 @@
             <button type="button" data-tf="false">False</button>
           </div>
           <p class="feedback"></p>
+          <div class="explain" hidden><p>The electrons stop in the target. What leaves the tube is an electromagnetic wave produced by the electrons' kinetic energy; it carries no charge, so an electric field cannot deflect it.</p></div>
         </div>
         <div class="tf-item" data-answer="false">
-          <p>(b) X-rays are produced by bombarding a metal target with high-frequency EM waves.</p>
+          <p>(b) X-rays are produced by bombarding a metal target with high-frequency electromagnetic waves.</p>
           <div class="row">
             <button type="button" data-tf="true">True</button>
             <button type="button" data-tf="false">False</button>
           </div>
           <p class="feedback"></p>
+          <div class="explain" hidden><p>The projectile is a fast electron, not a wave. The syllabus statement is "the emission of X-rays when fast electrons hit a heavy metal target".</p></div>
         </div>
-        <div class="tf-item" data-answer="true">
-          <p>(c) Exposure to X-rays is dangerous because they can damage cells.</p>
+        <div class="tf-item" data-answer="false">
+          <p>(c) In the tube the heavy metal target is connected to the negative terminal of the high-voltage supply.</p>
           <div class="row">
             <button type="button" data-tf="true">True</button>
             <button type="button" data-tf="false">False</button>
           </div>
           <p class="feedback"></p>
+          <div class="explain" hidden><p>Electrons are negative, so to be pulled towards the target the target must be the <em>positive</em> terminal (the anode). The hot filament that emits them is the negative terminal (the cathode). X-rays leave from the target, not from the filament.</p></div>
+        </div>
+      </div>
+      <div class="check" data-check="sa">
+        <h3>Write it out</h3>
+        <p>Describe briefly how X-rays are produced in an X-ray tube. <span class="marks">(3 marks)</span></p>
+        <button type="button" data-reveal>Show model answer</button>
+        <div class="model" hidden>
+          <ol>
+            <li>A filament is heated and emits electrons (thermionic emission).</li>
+            <li>A very high voltage between the filament and a heavy metal target accelerates the electrons to a high speed across the evacuated tube.</li>
+            <li>When the fast electrons hit the target they decelerate abruptly and part of their kinetic energy is emitted as X-rays (the rest becomes heat in the target).</li>
+          </ol>
+          <p class="note">Markers look for the three ideas in order: electron source, acceleration by a high voltage, fast electrons stopped by a heavy metal target. "Heated filament" alone or "electrons hit metal" alone scores one mark.</p>
         </div>
       </div>
     </section>
 
     <section class="idea" id="imaging">
       <header>
-        <h2>Flesh transmits, bone absorbs</h2>
-        <span class="stamp">Fig. 25.7</span>
+        <h2><span class="sec-num">E</span>Uses of X-rays</h2>
       </header>
-      <div class="visual play stage" id="imaging-vis" data-autoplay data-scene="imaging">
-        <canvas class="scene-canvas tall" width="960" height="480" aria-label="X-rays passing down through a hand onto photographic film"></canvas>
-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="xrays">X-rays</span>
-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="bone">bone</span>
-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="flesh">flesh</span>
-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="film">photographic film</span>
-      </div>
+      <figure class="fig">
+        <div class="visual play stage" id="imaging-vis" data-autoplay data-scene="imaging">
+          <canvas class="scene-canvas tall" width="960" height="480" aria-label="X-rays passing down through a hand onto photographic film"></canvas>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="xrays">X-rays</span>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="bone">bone</span>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="flesh">flesh</span>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="film">photographic film</span>
+        </div>
+        <figcaption><b>Fig. 25.7</b> Medical X-ray imaging. X-rays pass down through the hand onto a photographic film. Where flesh lets them through the film blackens; where bone absorbs them the film stays white.</figcaption>
+      </figure>
       <div class="row"><button type="button" data-replay="imaging-vis">Replay film</button></div>
-      <table class="compact pair">
-        <thead><tr><th>What you see</th><th>Therefore</th></tr></thead>
+      <table class="notes">
+        <thead><tr><th>Use</th><th>Which property is used</th></tr></thead>
         <tbody>
-          <tr><td>Flesh lets X-rays through; film under flesh goes dark</td><td>Soft tissue transmits</td></tr>
-          <tr><td>Bone stops X-rays; film under bone stays white</td><td>Bone absorbs, so bone looks white on the developed film</td></tr>
+          <tr><td>Medical radiography: bone fractures, teeth, chest; CT scans build a 3D image from many angles</td><td>Lower-frequency X-rays pass through flesh but are absorbed by bone and teeth, so bone appears white on the developed film and flesh dark</td></tr>
+          <tr><td>Industrial inspection of rails, welds, castings and pipes for hidden cracks; checking diamonds</td><td>Higher-frequency X-rays penetrate even bone and metal; a crack lets more through and shows as a darker line</td></tr>
+          <tr><td>Security screening of luggage and people at airports</td><td>Penetrating power differs with density: dense objects cast a shadow (some scanners image the reflected X-rays instead, so metal shows black)</td></tr>
+          <tr><td>Radiotherapy: killing cancer cells with a focused high-energy beam</td><td>Ionizing: X-rays damage the DNA of the cells they pass through</td></tr>
         </tbody>
       </table>
-      <!-- HKDSE 2022/31 -->
+      <div class="worked">
+        <span class="tag">Reading an X-ray image</span>
+        <p>Everything follows from one rule: the film blackens where X-rays reach it. The more a material absorbs, the whiter its shadow.</p>
+        <ul class="points">
+          <li>A metal pin holding a broken bone appears <b>whiter than the bone</b>: metal absorbs even more X-rays than bone.</li>
+          <li>A decayed (hollow) part of a tooth appears <b>darker</b> than healthy tooth: less material, so more X-rays get through. A metal filling appears <b>white</b>.</li>
+          <li>A pair of scissors placed on a wrapped film: the bare film goes darkest, the plastic handles leave a grey shadow, the steel blades leave a white shadow.</li>
+        </ul>
+      </div>
+      <aside class="tip">
+        <span class="tag">Exam tip: safe use</span>
+        <p>Because X-rays are ionizing, every use comes with precautions and you may be asked for them: confirm the patient is not pregnant (a foetus is easily damaged; ultrasound is used instead); remove metal objects, which block X-rays and spoil the image; use the lowest frequency and shortest exposure that give the image; cover other parts of the body with a lead apron; the radiographer stands behind a lead screen or leaves the room with the door closed, and only essential staff are present.</p>
+      </aside>
       <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="C">
-        <h3>Concept check</h3>
+        <h3 data-src="HKDSE 2022 Paper 1A Q31">Concept check</h3>
         <p>Which statement about X-rays is <strong>incorrect</strong>?</p>
         <div class="choices">
           <button type="button" data-choice="A">X-rays can be produced by high-speed electrons hitting a metal target.</button>
           <button type="button" data-choice="B">X-rays are a kind of electromagnetic wave.</button>
           <button type="button" data-choice="C">X-rays can be deflected by an electric field.</button>
-          <button type="button" data-choice="D">Although X-rays do not carry charges, they can cause ionization.</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="D">Although X-rays do not carry charge, they can cause ionization.</button>
         </div>
         <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p><b>C</b> is the false statement: an electric field only deflects charged particles, and X-rays carry no charge. <b>A</b> is how the tube works, <b>B</b> is their nature, and <b>D</b> is the key subtlety: ionization does not require the radiation itself to be charged, it requires enough energy per photon to remove an electron.</p>
+        </div>
       </div>
     </section>
 
     <section class="idea" id="make-xrays">
       <header>
-        <h2>Which hit makes X-rays?</h2>

       </header>

+      </figure>
       <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="D">
         <h3>Concept check</h3>
-        <p>UV light and fast-moving electrons hit sponge and heavy metal in turn. Where are X-rays produced?</p>
+        <p>In which set-up are X-rays produced?</p>
         <div class="choices">
-          <button type="button" data-choice="A">A</button>
-          <button type="button" data-choice="B">B</button>
-          <button type="button" data-choice="C">C</button>
-          <button type="button" data-choice="D">D</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="A">A: ultraviolet light on a sponge</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="B">B: ultraviolet light on a heavy metal block</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="C">C: fast electrons on a sponge</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="D">D: fast electrons on a heavy metal block</button>
         </div>
         <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p>Two conditions are needed: fast <em>electrons</em> as the projectile and a <em>heavy metal</em> target that stops them abruptly. <b>A</b> and <b>B</b> use a wave, not electrons, so they can at most warm or ionize the surface. <b>C</b> has electrons but a light, soft target that slows them gradually and produces essentially no X-rays.</p>
+        </div>
       </div>
     </section>
 
     <section class="idea" id="nuclear-vs-x">
       <header>
-        <h2>Nuclear radiation vs an X-ray switch</h2>
-        <span class="stamp">25.1 C</span>
+        <h2><span class="sec-num">G</span>Nuclear radiation is spontaneous; an X-ray beam is switched</h2>
       </header>
-      <div class="visual play stage" id="kinds-vis" data-scene="kinds">
-        <canvas class="scene-canvas" width="960" height="420" aria-label="Alpha helium nucleus, beta electron, gamma EM wave, X-ray tube"></canvas>
-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="alpha">α  He nucleus</span>
-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="beta">β  fast electron</span>
-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="gamma">γ  high-f EM</span>
-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="xray">X-ray: switched</span>
-      </div>
-      <table class="compact pair">
-        <thead><tr><th>What you see</th><th>Therefore</th></tr></thead>
+      <figure class="fig">
+        <div class="visual play stage" id="kinds-vis" data-scene="kinds">
+          <canvas class="scene-canvas" width="960" height="420" aria-label="Alpha helium nucleus, beta electron, gamma EM wave, X-ray tube"></canvas>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="alpha">α  He nucleus</span>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="beta">β  fast electron</span>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="gamma">γ  high-f EM</span>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="xray">X-ray: switched</span>
+        </div>
+        <figcaption>The three kinds of nuclear radiation leave the nucleus at random moments in random directions. An X-ray tube emits only while its power supply is on.</figcaption>
+      </figure>
+      <table class="notes">
+        <thead><tr><th></th><th>Nuclear radiation (α, β, γ)</th><th>X-rays</th></tr></thead>
         <tbody>
-          <tr><td>α, β, γ come from the nucleus, randomly in time and direction</td><td>Decay is spontaneous; you cannot switch it like an X-ray tube</td></tr>
-          <tr><td>An X-ray tube fires only while the gun is on</td><td>X-rays are switched, not a nuclear decay</td></tr>
+          <tr><th>Source</th><td>The nucleus of an unstable atom (a <b>radioactive</b> substance)</td><td>Fast electrons stopped by a metal target in a tube</td></tr>
+          <tr><th>Control</th><td>Spontaneous and random: cannot be switched off, sped up or slowed down by heating, pressure or chemical change</td><td>Switched on and off with the tube's power supply; intensity and penetrating power set by the current and voltage</td></tr>
+          <tr><th>Nature</th><td>α: helium nucleus (2p + 2n). β: fast electron. γ: very high-frequency EM wave</td><td>EM wave, like γ but produced outside the nucleus and usually of lower frequency</td></tr>
+          <tr><th>In common</th><td colspan="2">All are ionizing and invisible. γ rays and X-rays in particular are both EM waves: both travel at \( c \), cross a vacuum, carry no charge (so no field deflects them), and blacken photographic film. The <b>only</b> difference between a γ ray and an X-ray is its origin.</td></tr>
         </tbody>
       </table>
+      <div class="def">
+        <span class="tag">Definition</span>
+        <p>A substance is <b>radioactive</b> if it spontaneously emits α, β or γ radiation from unstable nuclei. The emission is called <b>radioactive decay</b>, and the radiation is called <b>nuclear radiation</b> because it comes from the nucleus. Becquerel discovered it in 1896 when uranium salts blackened a wrapped photographic plate, exactly as X-rays do. The names α, β and γ were given before their nature was known; Section 25.2 explains what each one is.</p>
+      </div>
+      <aside class="tip">
+        <span class="tag">Exam tip</span>
+        <p>If a question says radiation from a radioactive source passes <em>undeflected</em> through a field, it is γ, never "X-rays": a radioactive source does not emit X-rays, because X-rays are not nuclear radiation.</p>
+      </aside>
       <div class="check" data-check="tf">
         <h3>Concept check</h3>
         <div class="tf-item" data-answer="false">
-          <p>(a) All types of nuclear radiation are EM waves.</p>
+          <p>(a) All types of nuclear radiation are electromagnetic waves.</p>
           <div class="row"><button type="button" data-tf="true">True</button><button type="button" data-tf="false">False</button></div>
           <p class="feedback"></p>
+          <div class="explain" hidden><p>Only γ is an EM wave. α particles are helium nuclei and β particles are electrons; both have mass and charge.</p></div>
         </div>
         <div class="tf-item" data-answer="false">
-          <p>(b) Emission from uranium can be controlled like an X-ray tube.</p>
+          <p>(b) The emission of radiation from uranium can be switched on and off like an X-ray tube.</p>
           <div class="row"><button type="button" data-tf="true">True</button><button type="button" data-tf="false">False</button></div>
           <p class="feedback"></p>
+          <div class="explain" hidden><p>Decay is spontaneous. Nothing done to the atom from outside (heat, pressure, chemical reaction, electric field) changes when or whether its nucleus decays.</p></div>
         </div>
         <div class="tf-item" data-answer="false">
-          <p>(c) Nuclear radiation can be spread out by winds (as if it were a beam of air-borne stuff).</p>
+          <p>(c) After a nuclear accident, the "radiation" that the wind carries to neighbouring countries is α, β and γ radiation.</p>
           <div class="row"><button type="button" data-tf="true">True</button><button type="button" data-tf="false">False</button></div>
           <p class="feedback"></p>
+          <div class="explain" hidden><p>The radiation itself is energy (particles or waves) travelling in straight lines from the nucleus for at most a few metres in air (α and β) or until absorbed (γ). What wind can spread is radioactive <em>material</em>, the dust that contains the unstable nuclei, which then emits radiation wherever it lands. Keep "radiation" and "radioactive substance" separate.</p></div>
         </div>
       </div>
-      <!-- HKDSE 2015/31 -->
-      <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="A">
+      <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="B">
         <h3>Concept check</h3>
-        <p>Which nuclear reaction(s) is/are spontaneous?</p>
-        <p>(1) β decay of Na-24 &nbsp; (2) incoming neutron on a boron nucleus &nbsp; (3) fusion of hydrogen isotopes</p>
+        <p>Which of the following is/are a difference between γ rays and X-rays?</p>
+        <ol class="stem-list">
+          <li>γ rays are ionizing but X-rays are not.</li>
+          <li>γ rays are emitted from nuclei but X-rays are not.</li>
+          <li>X-rays are electromagnetic waves but γ rays are not.</li>
+        </ol>
+        <div class="choices">
+          <button type="button" data-choice="A">(1) only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="B">(2) only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="C">(1) and (3) only</button>

+        </div>
+        <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p>Only <b>(2)</b> is a real difference. Both are ionizing EM waves travelling at \( c \); a γ photon and an X-ray photon of the same frequency are indistinguishable. What differs is where they come from: a γ ray leaves an excited nucleus during radioactive decay (spontaneous), an X-ray is produced when fast electrons are stopped in a metal target (switchable).</p>
+        </div>
+      </div>
+      <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="A">
+        <h3 data-src="HKDSE 2015 Paper 1A Q31">Concept check</h3>
+        <p>Which of the following nuclear reactions is/are spontaneous?</p>
+        <ol class="stem-list">
+          <li>β decay of sodium-24</li>
+          <li>a boron nucleus absorbing an incoming neutron</li>
+          <li>fusion of hydrogen isotopes in a reactor</li>
+        </ol>
         <div class="choices">
           <button type="button" data-choice="A">(1) only</button>
           <button type="button" data-choice="B">(3) only</button>
@@ -316,14 +436,18 @@
           <button type="button" data-choice="D">(2) and (3) only</button>
         </div>
         <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p>"Spontaneous" means the nucleus changes on its own with nothing coming in. Only <b>(1)</b> qualifies: radioactive decay. <b>(2)</b> needs an incoming neutron and <b>(3)</b> needs the nuclei to be forced together at enormous temperature; both are induced reactions that stop when the supply of neutrons or heat stops.</p>
+        </div>

+      <div class="def">
+        <span class="tag">Definitions</span>
+        <p>Protons and neutrons are together called <b>nucleons</b>. The <b>atomic number</b> \( Z \) is the number of protons in the nucleus. The <b>mass number</b> \( A \) is the total number of nucleons, so the number of neutrons is \( N = A - Z \). A neutral atom has \( Z \) electrons, equal to its number of protons.</p>
+      </div>
+      <ul class="points">
+        <li>Almost all the mass is in the nucleus (nucleons are about 1840 times heavier than electrons) but almost all the volume is empty space: the atom is about \( 10^{-10}\ \mathrm{m} \) across, the nucleus \( 10^{-15} \) to \( 10^{-14}\ \mathrm{m} \). A nucleus in an atom is like a grain of sand in a football stadium.</li>
+        <li>The element is fixed by \( Z \) alone: every nucleus with 6 protons is carbon, whatever its number of neutrons.</li>
+      </ul>
+      <div class="check" data-check="tf">
+        <h3>Concept check</h3>
+        <div class="tf-item" data-answer="true">
+          <p>(a) The volume of an atom is mostly empty space.</p>
+          <div class="row"><button type="button" data-tf="true">True</button><button type="button" data-tf="false">False</button></div>
+          <p class="feedback"></p>
+          <div class="explain" hidden><p>The nucleus is about \( 10^{4} \) times smaller across than the atom, so it occupies about \( 10^{-12} \) of the atom's volume; the electrons are point-like. Almost everything in between is empty.</p></div>
+        </div>
+        <div class="tf-item" data-answer="false">
+          <p>(b) Because the nucleus is so tiny, it holds only a small proportion of the atom's mass.</p>
+          <div class="row"><button type="button" data-tf="true">True</button><button type="button" data-tf="false">False</button></div>
+          <p class="feedback"></p>
+          <div class="explain" hidden><p>Size and mass are different things. One nucleon has about 1840 times the mass of an electron, so the nucleus holds more than 99.9% of the atom's mass in a tiny volume; it is extremely dense.</p></div>
+        </div>
+      </div>
     </section>
 
     <section class="idea" id="notation">
       <header>
-        <h2>Nuclide notation</h2>
-        <span class="stamp">Fig. 25.12 · Table 25.2</span>
+        <h2><span class="sec-num">B</span>Nuclide notation and isotopes</h2>
       </header>
-      <div class="visual play stage" id="nuclide-vis" data-scene="nuclide">
-        <canvas class="scene-canvas" width="960" height="420" aria-label="Nuclide with nucleons and electron shells"></canvas>

-      <p class="caption" id="nuclide-counts"></p>

-    <section class="idea" id="hydrogen">
-      <header>
-        <h2>Add neutrons, Z stays 1</h2>
-        <span class="stamp">Fig. 25.13</span>
-      </header>
-      <div class="visual play stage" id="iso-vis" data-scene="hydrogen">
-        <canvas class="scene-canvas" width="960" height="420" aria-label="Hydrogen isotopes"></canvas>
-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="iso">¹H</span>

-      <p class="caption" id="iso-label">¹H  protium  N = 0  ·  Z = 1</p>
-      <div class="row">
+        <span class="sep" aria-hidden="true"></span>
         <button type="button" data-iso="0">¹H</button>
         <button type="button" data-iso="1">²H</button>
         <button type="button" data-iso="2">³H</button>
       </div>
-      <div class="check" data-check="tf">
-        <h3>Concept check</h3>
-        <div class="tf-item" data-answer="true">
-          <p>The volume of an atom is mostly empty space.</p>
-          <div class="row"><button type="button" data-tf="true">True</button><button type="button" data-tf="false">False</button></div>
-          <p class="feedback"></p>
-        </div>
-        <div class="tf-item" data-answer="false">
-          <p>The nucleus forms only a small proportion of the total mass because it is tiny.</p>
-          <div class="row"><button type="button" data-tf="true">True</button><button type="button" data-tf="false">False</button></div>
-          <p class="feedback"></p>
-        </div>
+      <p class="caption" id="nuclide-counts"></p>
+      <p class="caption" id="iso-label">¹H  protium  N = 0  ·  Z = 1</p>
+      <div class="def">
+        <span class="tag">Definitions</span>
+        <p>A <b>nuclide</b> is a species of nucleus with a definite \( Z \) and \( A \), written \( {}^{A}_{Z}\mathrm{X} \): mass number on top, atomic number below, e.g. \( {}^{12}_{6}\mathrm{C} \) or \( {}^{238}_{92}\mathrm{U} \). <b>Isotopes</b> are nuclides of the same element (same \( Z \), same number of protons) with different numbers of neutrons (different \( A \)).</p>
+      </div>
+      <ul class="points">
+        <li>Isotopes of an element have the same chemical properties (same electrons) and slightly different physical properties (different mass). Natural carbon is about 99% \( {}^{12}\mathrm{C} \), 1% \( {}^{13}\mathrm{C} \) and a trace of \( {}^{14}\mathrm{C} \); only \( {}^{14}\mathrm{C} \) is radioactive. \( {}^{2}\mathrm{H} \) is called deuterium and \( {}^{3}\mathrm{H} \) tritium.</li>
+        <li>Some isotopes of every element are unstable. Nuclei with \( Z > 83 \) (heavier than bismuth) are all unstable: the strong nuclear force that binds nucleons acts only over a very short range, and in a large nucleus the electrical repulsion between the many protons wins.</li>
+      </ul>
+      <div class="worked">
+        <span class="tag">Worked example</span>
+        <p>Three nuclides are \( {}^{14}_{6}\mathrm{P} \), \( {}^{12}_{6}\mathrm{Q} \) and \( {}^{14}_{7}\mathrm{R} \). (a) How many elements are present? (b) Which nuclide has the fewest neutrons? (c) Which neutral atoms have the fewest electrons?</p>
+        <ol class="steps">
+          <li>(a) The element is fixed by \( Z \). P and Q both have \( Z = 6 \) (isotopes of one element); R has \( Z = 7 \). Two elements.</li>
+          <li>(b) \( N = A - Z \): P has 8, Q has 6, R has 7. Q has the fewest neutrons.</li>
+          <li>(c) Electrons in a neutral atom equal \( Z \): P and Q have 6 each, R has 7. P and Q.</li>
+        </ol>
       </div>
-      <!-- HKDSE 2012/36 -->

         <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p>For isotopes \( Z \) is fixed, so \( N = A - Z \): a straight line of slope 1 that meets the \( A \)-axis at \( A = Z \) (a nucleus with no neutrons). <b>D</b>. <b>A</b> passes through the origin, which would mean \( N = A \), forgetting the protons. <b>B</b> has \( N \) falling as \( A \) rises, impossible when each extra nucleon is a neutron. <b>C</b> keeps \( N \) constant while \( A \) changes, which would require \( Z \) to change, i.e. a different element.</p>
+        </div>
+      </div>
+      <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="C">
+        <h3>Concept check</h3>
+        <p>Consider the nuclides \( {}^{40}_{18}\mathrm{Ar} \), \( {}^{40}_{19}\mathrm{K} \), \( {}^{40}_{20}\mathrm{Ca} \) and \( {}^{42}_{20}\mathrm{Ca} \). Which of the following statements is/are correct?</p>
+        <ol class="stem-list">
+          <li>\( {}^{40}\mathrm{Ar} \), \( {}^{40}\mathrm{K} \) and \( {}^{40}\mathrm{Ca} \) are isotopes because they have the same mass number.</li>
+          <li>\( {}^{40}\mathrm{Ca} \) and \( {}^{42}\mathrm{Ca} \) are isotopes.</li>
+          <li>\( {}^{40}\mathrm{Ar} \) and \( {}^{42}\mathrm{Ca} \) have the same number of neutrons.</li>
+        </ol>
+        <div class="choices">
+          <button type="button" data-choice="A">(1) only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="B">(2) only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="C">(2) and (3) only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="D">(1), (2) and (3)</button>
+        </div>
+        <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p>Isotopes share \( Z \), not \( A \). (1) is the classic trap: three different elements happen to have \( A = 40 \); they are not isotopes. (2) is correct: same \( Z = 20 \), different \( A \). (3): \( N = 40 - 18 = 22 \) for argon-40 and \( 42 - 20 = 22 \) for calcium-42, so it is also correct. Answer <b>C</b>.</p>
+        </div>
       </div>
     </section>
 
     <section class="idea" id="decay-anim">
       <header>
-        <h2>Three ways an unstable nucleus sheds energy</h2>
-        <span class="stamp">Fig. 25.14-25.17</span>
+        <h2><span class="sec-num">C</span>Three ways an unstable nucleus sheds energy</h2>
       </header>
-      <div class="row">
-        <button type="button" data-decay="a">α of ²⁰⁸Po</button>
-        <button type="button" data-decay="b">β of ⁶⁰Co</button>
-        <button type="button" data-decay="g">γ of ⁶⁰Ni*</button>
+      <div class="def">
+        <span class="tag">Vocabulary</span>
+        <p>An unstable nuclide (a <b>radionuclide</b> or <b>radioisotope</b>) gets rid of excess energy by emitting radiation; this is <b>radioactive decay</b>. The original nucleus is the <b>parent</b>, the nucleus left behind is the <b>daughter</b>. When the daughter is a different element (different \( Z \)) the change is a <b>transmutation</b>. Decay is spontaneous and random: nothing outside the nucleus (heat, pressure, chemical bonding) starts, stops, speeds or slows it.</p>
       </div>
-      <div class="visual play stage" id="decay-a" data-decay-panel="a" data-autoplay data-scene="decay-a">
-        <canvas class="scene-canvas" width="960" height="420" aria-label="Alpha particle leaves the polonium-208 nucleus"></canvas>
-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="parent">²⁰⁸₈₄Po</span>
-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="ejectile">²⁰⁴₈₂Pb + ⁴₂He</span>
-        <span class="hud-label" style="left:72%;top:78%">A − 4 · Z − 2</span>
-        <div class="eq">\[ {}^{A}_{Z}\mathrm{X} \rightarrow {}^{A-4}_{Z-2}\mathrm{Y} + {}^{4}_{2}\mathrm{He} \]</div>
-      </div>
-      <div class="visual play stage" id="decay-b" data-decay-panel="b" hidden data-scene="decay-b">
-        <canvas class="scene-canvas" width="960" height="420" aria-label="Beta electron leaves the cobalt-60 nucleus"></canvas>
-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="parent">⁶⁰₂₇Co</span>
-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="ejectile">⁶⁰₂₈Ni + ⁰₋₁e</span>
-        <span class="hud-label" style="left:72%;top:78%">n → p + e⁻ inside the nucleus</span>
-        <div class="eq">\[ {}^{A}_{Z}\mathrm{X} \rightarrow {}^{A}_{Z+1}\mathrm{Y} + {}^{0}_{-1}e \]</div>
-      </div>
-      <div class="visual play stage" id="decay-g" data-decay-panel="g" hidden data-scene="decay-g">
-        <canvas class="scene-canvas" width="960" height="420" aria-label="Gamma pulse leaves excited nickel-60"></canvas>
-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="parent">⁶⁰₂₈Ni*</span>
-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="ejectile">⁶⁰₂₈Ni + γ</span>
-        <span class="hud-label" style="left:72%;top:78%">not a transmutation · A, Z unchanged</span>
-        <div class="eq">\[ {}^{A}_{Z}\mathrm{X}^{*} \rightarrow {}^{A}_{Z}\mathrm{X} + \gamma \]</div>
-      </div>
-      <table class="compact pair">
-        <thead><tr><th>What you see</th><th>Therefore</th></tr></thead>
+      <figure class="fig">
+        <div class="row tabs" role="tablist" aria-label="Choose a decay to animate">
+          <button type="button" data-decay="a" aria-pressed="true">α decay of ²⁰⁸Po</button>
+          <button type="button" data-decay="b">β decay of ⁶⁰Co</button>
+          <button type="button" data-decay="g">γ emission from ⁶⁰Ni*</button>
+        </div>
+        <div class="visual play stage" id="decay-a" data-decay-panel="a" data-autoplay data-scene="decay-a">
+          <canvas class="scene-canvas" width="960" height="420" aria-label="Alpha particle leaves the polonium-208 nucleus"></canvas>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="parent">²⁰⁸₈₄Po</span>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="ejectile">²⁰⁴₈₂Pb + ⁴₂He</span>
+          <span class="hud-label" style="left:72%;top:78%">A − 4 · Z − 2</span>
+          <div class="eq">\[ {}^{A}_{Z}\mathrm{X} \rightarrow {}^{A-4}_{Z-2}\mathrm{Y} + {}^{4}_{2}\mathrm{He} \]</div>
+        </div>
+        <div class="visual play stage" id="decay-b" data-decay-panel="b" hidden data-scene="decay-b">
+          <canvas class="scene-canvas" width="960" height="420" aria-label="Beta electron leaves the cobalt-60 nucleus"></canvas>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="parent">⁶⁰₂₇Co</span>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="ejectile">⁶⁰₂₈Ni + ⁰₋₁e</span>
+          <span class="hud-label" style="left:72%;top:78%">n → p + e⁻ inside the nucleus</span>
+          <div class="eq">\[ {}^{A}_{Z}\mathrm{X} \rightarrow {}^{A}_{Z+1}\mathrm{Y} + {}^{0}_{-1}e \]</div>
+        </div>
+        <div class="visual play stage" id="decay-g" data-decay-panel="g" hidden data-scene="decay-g">
+          <canvas class="scene-canvas" width="960" height="420" aria-label="Gamma pulse leaves excited nickel-60"></canvas>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="parent">⁶⁰₂₈Ni*</span>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="ejectile">⁶⁰₂₈Ni + γ</span>
+          <span class="hud-label" style="left:72%;top:78%">not a transmutation · A, Z unchanged</span>
+          <div class="eq">\[ {}^{A}_{Z}\mathrm{X}^{*} \rightarrow {}^{A}_{Z}\mathrm{X} + \gamma \]</div>
+        </div>
+        <figcaption><b>Fig. 25.14 to 25.17</b> The three decays. Choose one above; each animation loops.</figcaption>
+      </figure>
+      <table class="notes">
+        <thead><tr><th>Decay</th><th>What leaves</th><th>What happens inside the nucleus</th><th>Change in \( A \)</th><th>Change in \( Z \)</th><th>Change in \( N \)</th></tr></thead>
         <tbody>
-          <tr><td>β electron leaves the nucleus</td><td>It came from a neutron, not from an orbit</td></tr>
-          <tr><td>ΣA and ΣZ match on both sides</td><td>Nucleon number and charge are conserved</td></tr>
+          <tr><td><b>α</b></td><td>A helium nucleus \( {}^{4}_{2}\mathrm{He} \): 2 protons + 2 neutrons</td><td>A tightly bound group of four nucleons is ejected</td><td>−4</td><td>−2</td><td>−2</td></tr>
+          <tr><td><b>β</b></td><td>A fast electron \( {}^{0}_{-1}e \) (also written \( {}^{0}_{-1}\beta \))</td><td>One neutron turns into a proton, which stays, and an electron, which is emitted. The electron is created in the nucleus; it is <em>not</em> one of the orbital electrons</td><td>0</td><td>+1</td><td>−1</td></tr>
+          <tr><td><b>γ</b></td><td>A very high-frequency EM wave, no mass, no charge</td><td>After an α or β decay the daughter is often left with excess energy (an <em>excited</em> nucleus, marked *). It drops to its normal state by emitting the energy as a γ ray</td><td>0</td><td>0</td><td>0</td></tr>
         </tbody>
       </table>
+      <ul class="points">
+        <li><b>Conservation.</b> In every equation the total mass number and the total atomic number (charge) are the same on both sides. This is how you balance an unknown.</li>
+        <li><b>Consequences.</b> Any α or β decay produces a different element, so it is always a transmutation. γ emission changes neither \( A \) nor \( Z \); the nuclide only loses energy. That is why a source such as Ra-226 or Am-241 is listed as emitting "α and γ": the γ follows the α from the excited daughter.</li>
+        <li><b>Answer format.</b> If asked "state the kind of radiation", write "α particle" or "β particle", not "α decay". In β decay the particle emitted is an electron; no neutron is emitted.</li>
+      </ul>
+      <div class="worked">
+        <span class="tag">Worked example</span>
+        <p>Write the equation for (a) the α decay of radium-226 (\( Z = 88 \)) to radon, and (b) the β decay of carbon-14 (\( Z = 6 \)) to nitrogen.</p>
+        <ol class="steps">
+          <li>(a) Put the parent on the left, the daughter and the particle on the right; balance top and bottom: \( 226 = A + 4 \) gives \( A = 222 \); \( 88 = Z + 2 \) gives \( Z = 86 \).
+            <div class="eq">\[ {}^{226}_{88}\mathrm{Ra} \rightarrow {}^{222}_{86}\mathrm{Rn} + {}^{4}_{2}\mathrm{He} \]</div></li>
+          <li>(b) The electron has \( A = 0 \), \( Z = -1 \): \( 14 = A + 0 \) gives \( A = 14 \); \( 6 = Z + (-1) \) gives \( Z = 7 \).
+            <div class="eq">\[ {}^{14}_{6}\mathrm{C} \rightarrow {}^{14}_{7}\mathrm{N} + {}^{0}_{-1}e \]</div></li>
+        </ol>
+      </div>
+      <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="B">
+        <h3>Concept check</h3>
+        <p>Which equation correctly represents the β decay of lead-214, \( {}^{214}_{82}\mathrm{Pb} \)?</p>
+        <div class="choices">
+          <button type="button" data-choice="A">\( {}^{214}_{82}\mathrm{Pb} + {}^{0}_{-1}e \rightarrow {}^{214}_{81}\mathrm{X} \)</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="B">\( {}^{214}_{82}\mathrm{Pb} \rightarrow {}^{214}_{83}\mathrm{X} + {}^{0}_{-1}e \)</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="C">\( {}^{214}_{82}\mathrm{Pb} \rightarrow {}^{213}_{82}\mathrm{X} + {}^{0}_{-1}e \)</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="D">\( {}^{214}_{82}\mathrm{Pb} \rightarrow {}^{214}_{81}\mathrm{X} + {}^{0}_{-1}e \)</button>
+        </div>
+        <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p><b>B</b>: the electron is emitted (right-hand side) and the totals balance: \( 214 = 214 + 0 \), \( 82 = 83 + (-1) \). <b>A</b> puts the electron on the left as if it were absorbed. <b>C</b> lowers \( A \), but an electron has zero mass number. <b>D</b> lowers \( Z \), forgetting that the electron's charge is −1, so the daughter must have one <em>more</em> proton.</p>
+        </div>
+      </div>
+      <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="C">
+        <h3>Concept check</h3>
+        <p>A nuclide R undergoes β decay, and the daughter then emits a γ ray. Which of the following statements is/are correct?</p>
+        <ol class="stem-list">
+          <li>After the β decay the nucleus has one more proton and one fewer neutron.</li>
+          <li>The β particle is one of the orbital electrons of the atom R.</li>
+          <li>The γ emission leaves the mass number and atomic number unchanged.</li>
+        </ol>
+        <div class="choices">
+          <button type="button" data-choice="A">(1) only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="B">(2) only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="C">(1) and (3) only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="D">(1), (2) and (3)</button>
+        </div>
+        <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p>(1) is what β decay is: a neutron becomes a proton plus an electron, so \( Z \) rises by 1, \( N \) falls by 1 and \( A \) is unchanged. (3) is right: a γ ray carries away energy only. (2) is the common misconception; the electron is created inside the nucleus at the moment of decay. Answer <b>C</b>.</p>
+        </div>
+      </div>
+      <div class="check" data-check="sa">
+        <h3>Write it out</h3>
+        <p>Bismuth-213 (\( Z = 83 \)) undergoes β decay to polonium (Po), which then undergoes α decay to lead (Pb). Write the two decay equations. <span class="marks">(HKDSE 2026 Paper 1B Q12, 2 marks)</span></p>
+        <button type="button" data-reveal>Show model answer</button>
+        <div class="model" hidden>
+          <div class="eq">\[ {}^{213}_{83}\mathrm{Bi} \rightarrow {}^{213}_{84}\mathrm{Po} + {}^{0}_{-1}e \]</div>
+          <div class="eq">\[ {}^{213}_{84}\mathrm{Po} \rightarrow {}^{209}_{82}\mathrm{Pb} + {}^{4}_{2}\mathrm{He} \]</div>
+          <p class="note">One mark for each equation with both numbers correct on every symbol. Check each line: top row 213 = 213 + 0 and 213 = 209 + 4; bottom row 83 = 84 − 1 and 84 = 82 + 2.</p>
+        </div>
+      </div>
     </section>
 
     <section class="idea" id="series">
       <header>
-        <h2>U-238 series on A-Z and N-Z</h2>
-        <span class="stamp">Fig. 25.18</span>
+        <h2><span class="sec-num">D</span>Decay series</h2>
       </header>
-      <div class="visual">
-        <svg viewBox="0 0 640 250" role="img" aria-label="Decay series plots">
-          <g id="series-az">
-            <line x1="48" y1="208" x2="292" y2="208" stroke="#333"/>
-            <line x1="48" y1="208" x2="48" y2="22" stroke="#333"/>
-            <text x="154" y="236" font-size="12">Z</text>
-            <text x="8" y="28" font-size="12">A</text>
-            <text x="40" y="222" font-size="10">82</text>

-          </g>
-          <g id="series-nz" transform="translate(320,0)">
-            <line x1="48" y1="208" x2="292" y2="208" stroke="#333"/>
-            <line x1="48" y1="208" x2="48" y2="22" stroke="#333"/>

-            <text x="8" y="214" font-size="10">124</text>
-            <text x="8" y="36" font-size="10">146</text>
-          </g>
-        </svg>
-      </div>
+      <p>The daughter of a decay is often itself unstable and decays again. The chain continues until a stable nuclide is reached. Uranium-238 goes through 14 decays (8 α and 6 β) before ending at stable lead-206: \( {}^{238}\mathrm{U} \xrightarrow{\alpha} {}^{234}\mathrm{Th} \xrightarrow{\beta} {}^{234}\mathrm{Pa} \xrightarrow{\beta} {}^{234}\mathrm{U} \xrightarrow{\alpha} \cdots \rightarrow {}^{206}\mathrm{Pb} \). Members of a series with the same \( Z \) are isotopes of one another.</p>
+      <figure class="fig">
+        <div class="visual">
+          <svg viewBox="0 0 640 250" role="img" aria-label="Decay series plots">
+            <g id="series-az">
+              <line x1="48" y1="208" x2="292" y2="208" stroke="#333"/>
+              <line x1="48" y1="208" x2="48" y2="22" stroke="#333"/>
+              <text x="154" y="236" font-size="12">Z</text>
+              <text x="8" y="28" font-size="12">A</text>
+              <text x="40" y="222" font-size="10">82</text>
+              <text x="268" y="222" font-size="10">92</text>
+              <text x="8" y="214" font-size="10">206</text>
+              <text x="8" y="36" font-size="10">238</text>
+            </g>
+            <g id="series-nz" transform="translate(320,0)">
+              <line x1="48" y1="208" x2="292" y2="208" stroke="#333"/>
+              <line x1="48" y1="208" x2="48" y2="22" stroke="#333"/>
+              <text x="154" y="236" font-size="12">Z</text>

+              <text x="268" y="222" font-size="10">92</text>
+              <text x="8" y="214" font-size="10">124</text>
+              <text x="8" y="36" font-size="10">146</text>
+            </g>
+          </svg>
+        </div>
+        <figcaption><b>Fig. 25.18</b> The U-238 series plotted on an \( A \)-\( Z \) chart (left) and an \( N \)-\( Z \) chart (right). Step through the chain with the button; the readout gives the current member.</figcaption>
+      </figure>
       <div class="row">
         <button type="button" id="series-next">Step α / β</button>
         <button type="button" id="series-reset">Reset to U-238</button>
       </div>
       <p class="caption" id="series-readout"></p>
-      <div class="legend">
-        <span><i class="swatch a"></i>α: A − 4, Z − 2</span>
-        <span><i class="swatch b"></i>β: A same, Z + 1</span>
-        <span><i class="swatch g"></i>γ: invisible on these axes</span>
+      <table class="notes">
+        <thead><tr><th>Decay</th><th>On an \( A \)-\( Z \) chart</th><th>On an \( N \)-\( Z \) chart</th></tr></thead>
+        <tbody>
+          <tr><td>α</td><td>down 4, left 2</td><td>down 2, left 2 (diagonal)</td></tr>
+          <tr><td>β</td><td>right 1, same height</td><td>down 1, right 1 (diagonal the other way)</td></tr>
+          <tr><td>γ</td><td colspan="2">no move: the point stays where it is</td></tr>
+        </tbody>
+      </table>
+      <div class="worked" id="pu239">
+        <span class="tag">Worked example: counting α and β</span>
+        <p>Plutonium-239 decays through a series to lead-207. How many α and how many β decays are involved?</p>
+        <div class="eq">\[ {}^{239}_{94}\mathrm{Pu} \rightarrow {}^{207}_{82}\mathrm{Pb} \]</div>
+        <ol class="steps">
+          <li>Only α changes \( A \), by 4 each time: \(\Delta A = 239 - 207 = 32\) → n<sub>α</sub> = 32 / 4 = 8</li>
+          <li>Those 8 α alone would take \( Z \) to \( 94 - 2 \times 8 = 78 \).</li>
+          <li>Each β raises \( Z \) by 1 and the final \( Z \) is 82, so n<sub>β</sub> = 82 − 78 = 4</li>
+        </ol>
+        <p>Write it in exam form as two equations: \( 4n_{\alpha} = \Delta A \) and \( 2n_{\alpha} - n_{\beta} = \Delta Z \).</p>
       </div>
-    </section>
-
-    <section class="idea" id="pu239">
-      <header>
-        <h2>Worked example · count α then β</h2>
-        <span class="stamp">Worked example</span>
-      </header>
-      <div class="eq">\[ {}^{239}_{94}\mathrm{Pu} \rightarrow {}^{207}_{82}\mathrm{Pb} \]</div>
-      <p class="eq">\(\Delta A = 239 - 207 = 32\) → n<sub>α</sub> = 32 / 4 = 8</p>
-      <p class="eq">Z after only \(\alpha\): \(94 - 2 \times 8 = 78\)</p>
-      <p class="eq">Need \(Z = 82\), so n<sub>β</sub> = 82 − 78 = 4</p>
+      <aside class="tip">
+        <span class="tag">Exam tip: which nuclides can belong to a series?</span>
+        <p>Because only α changes \( A \), and by 4 each time, every member of a series has a mass number that differs from the parent's by a multiple of 4. A candidate with the wrong \( A \) can be ruled out immediately. Then check \( Z \): after \( n_{\alpha} \) alphas and \( n_{\beta} \) betas, \( Z = Z_0 - 2n_{\alpha} + n_{\beta} \) with \( n_{\beta} \geq 0 \). Also: "state the end product of the Ra-226 series from Pb-206, Pb-207, Pb-208" is answered by \( 226 - 206 = 20 \), a multiple of 4.</p>
+      </aside>
       <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="B">
-        <h3>How many β decays?</h3>
+        <h3>Concept check</h3>
+        <p>Neptunium-237, \( {}^{237}_{93}\mathrm{Np} \), decays through a series to stable bismuth-209, \( {}^{209}_{83}\mathrm{Bi} \). The numbers of α and β decays are</p>
         <div class="choices">
-          <button type="button" data-choice="A">3</button>
-          <button type="button" data-choice="B">4</button>
-          <button type="button" data-choice="C">5</button>
-          <button type="button" data-choice="D">6</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="A">6 α and 5 β</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="B">7 α and 4 β</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="C">7 α and 3 β</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="D">8 α and 6 β</button>
         </div>
         <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p>\( \Delta A = 237 - 209 = 28 = 4 \times 7 \), so 7 α. After them \( Z = 93 - 14 = 79 \); to reach 83 needs 4 β. <b>B</b>. <b>A</b> and <b>D</b> get the α count wrong (28 is not 24 or 32). <b>C</b> subtracts the \( Z \) values directly (\( 93 - 83 = 10 \), then confuses the bookkeeping) instead of first applying the α step.</p>
+        </div>
       </div>
-    </section>

-    <section class="idea" id="arith">
-      <header>
-        <h2>α then ββ arithmetic</h2>
-        <span class="stamp">Series bookkeeping</span>
-      </header>
       <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="A">
-        <h3>U-238 undergoes α-β-β-α. Atomic number and mass number of X?</h3>
+        <h3 data-src="HKDSE 2013 Paper 1A Q34">Concept check</h3>
+        <p>A uranium-238 nucleus \( {}^{238}_{92}\mathrm{U} \) undergoes an α decay, then two β decays, then another α decay, to become nuclide X. What are the atomic number and mass number of X?</p>
         <div class="choices">
           <button type="button" data-choice="A">90 and 230</button>
           <button type="button" data-choice="B">90 and 234</button>
@@ -294,10 +409,18 @@
           <button type="button" data-choice="D">88 and 234</button>
         </div>
         <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p>Two α: \( A \) falls by 8 to 230 and \( Z \) by 4 to 88. Two β: \( Z \) rises by 2 to 90, \( A \) unchanged. So (90, 230), <b>A</b>. <b>B</b> and <b>D</b> forget the second α in \( A \); <b>C</b> forgets that β raises \( Z \).</p>
+        </div>
       </div>
       <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="D">
-        <h3>W --α--> X --β--> Y --β--> Z. Which is/are correct?</h3>
-        <p>(1) X has 1 more proton than Y. (2) W has 2 more neutrons than X. (3) W and Z are isotopes.</p>
+        <h3 data-src="HKDSE 2014 Paper 1A Q31">Concept check</h3>
+        <p>A nuclide W decays as follows: W → X (α decay), X → Y (β decay), Y → Z (β decay). Which of the following statements is/are correct?</p>
+        <ol class="stem-list">
+          <li>X has one more proton than Y.</li>
+          <li>W has two more neutrons than X.</li>
+          <li>W and Z are isotopes.</li>
+        </ol>
         <div class="choices">
           <button type="button" data-choice="A">(1) only</button>
           <button type="button" data-choice="B">(2) only</button>
@@ -305,83 +428,161 @@
           <button type="button" data-choice="D">(2) and (3) only</button>
         </div>
         <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p>Track \( (Z, N) \). α: \( (Z-2, N-2) \); each β: \( (Z+1, N-1) \). So X = \( (Z-2, N-2) \), Y = \( (Z-1, N-3) \), Z = \( (Z, N-4) \). (1) X has one <em>fewer</em> proton than Y, false. (2) true: W has two more neutrons than X. (3) true: W and Z have the same \( Z \) (and \( A \) differs by 4), so they are isotopes. Answer <b>D</b>.</p>
+        </div>
+      </div>
+      <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="B">
+        <h3>Concept check</h3>
+        <p>Radium-226, \( {}^{226}_{88}\mathrm{Ra} \), decays by a series of α and β decays. Which of the following nuclides <strong>cannot</strong> be a member of its series?</p>
+        <div class="choices">
+          <button type="button" data-choice="A">\( Z = 86,\ A = 222 \)</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="B">\( Z = 84,\ A = 216 \)</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="C">\( Z = 83,\ A = 214 \)</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="D">\( Z = 82,\ A = 210 \)</button>
+        </div>
+        <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p>\( A \) must fall in steps of 4 from 226. <b>B</b> has \( 226 - 216 = 10 \), not a multiple of 4, so it is impossible whatever the β count. The others pass: <b>A</b> is one α; <b>C</b> is 3 α (\( Z = 82 \)) then 1 β; <b>D</b> is 4 α (\( Z = 80 \)) then 2 β. (This is the reasoning behind HKDSE 2025 Paper 1A Q32.)</p>
+        </div>
       </div>
       <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="B">
-        <h3>Bi-213 → Po-213 → Pb-209</h3>
-        <p>A unchanged, then A − 4.</p>
+        <h3 data-src="after HKDSE 2018 Paper 1B Q10">Concept check</h3>
+        <p>A school radium-226 source is described as an α emitter, yet a detector near it also registers β and γ radiation. The best explanation is that</p>
         <div class="choices">
-          <button type="button" data-choice="A">α then β</button>
-          <button type="button" data-choice="B">β then α</button>
-          <button type="button" data-choice="C">γ then α</button>
-          <button type="button" data-choice="D">α then α</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="A">the source is impure and contains other elements.</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="B">daughter nuclides in the radium decay series (e.g. Pb-214, Bi-214) are themselves radioactive and emit β and γ.</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="C">α particles turn into β particles when they hit the metal casing.</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="D">the extra readings are only background radiation.</button>
         </div>
         <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p><b>B</b>. Ra-226 is part of the U-238 series; its daughters Rn-222, Po-218, Pb-214, Bi-214 and so on accumulate inside the sealed source and several of them are β or γ emitters. The examiners' report for this question singled out <b>A</b> as the wrong answer many candidates gave. <b>C</b> describes no real process, and <b>D</b> cannot explain readings far above background.</p>
+        </div>
       </div>
     </section>
 
     <section class="idea" id="lab">
       <header>
-        <h2>Sealed source and handling</h2>
-        <span class="stamp">Fig. 25.19 · Table 25.3</span>
+        <h2><span class="sec-num">E</span>Radioactive sources in the laboratory and how to handle them</h2>
       </header>
-      <div class="visual play stage" id="lab-vis" data-scene="sealed">
-        <canvas class="scene-canvas" width="960" height="420" aria-label="Sealed source: metal capsule, mesh window, material inside"></canvas>
-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="case">metal case</span>
-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="material">radioactive material</span>
-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="mesh">mesh</span>
-      </div>
-      <table class="compact">
-        <thead><tr><th>source</th><th>types</th></tr></thead>
+      <figure class="fig">
+        <div class="visual play stage" id="lab-vis" data-scene="sealed">
+          <canvas class="scene-canvas" width="960" height="420" aria-label="Sealed source: metal capsule, mesh window, material inside"></canvas>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="case">metal case</span>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="material">radioactive material</span>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="mesh">mesh</span>
+        </div>
+        <figcaption><b>Fig. 25.19</b> A sealed source: a small quantity of radioactive material fixed inside a metal case that shields all directions except a wire-mesh window. Radiation leaves only through the window, so a source must never be pointed at anyone.</figcaption>
+      </figure>
+      <ul class="points">
+        <li>Schools may use only weak <b>sealed sources</b> like this one. Radioactive material that is not enclosed (rock samples, radon gas) is an <b>open source</b>.</li>
+        <li>Radiation from the source cannot make the room, the bench or the student radioactive (see irradiation vs contamination in Section F). The danger is the dose of ionizing radiation absorbed while the source is out.</li>
+      </ul>
+      <table class="notes" aria-label="Table 25.3">
+        <thead><tr><th>Common school source</th><th>Radiation emitted</th></tr></thead>
         <tbody>
-          <tr><td>Ra-226</td><td>α, β and γ</td></tr>
-          <tr><td>Am-241</td><td>α and γ (small amount)</td></tr>
-          <tr><td>Sr-90</td><td>β</td></tr>
-          <tr><td>Co-60</td><td>γ</td></tr>
+          <tr><td>Radium-226</td><td>α, β and γ (β and much of the γ come from daughters in its series)</td></tr>
+          <tr><td>Americium-241</td><td>α and a small amount of γ</td></tr>
+          <tr><td>Strontium-90</td><td>β</td></tr>
+          <tr><td>Cobalt-60</td><td>β and γ (the β from Co-60, the γ from the excited Ni-60 daughter); used as a γ source behind an absorber</td></tr>
         </tbody>
       </table>
-      <table class="compact pair">
-        <thead><tr><th>Do</th><th>Why</th></tr></thead>
+      <table class="notes">
+        <thead><tr><th>Precaution</th><th>Reason</th></tr></thead>
         <tbody>
-          <tr><td>Forceps; do not point at anyone</td><td>Keep distance and aim away from people</td></tr>
-          <tr><td>Lead box, locked; wash hands; warning signs</td><td>Store, decontaminate, and mark the source</td></tr>
+          <tr><td>Handle only with long forceps or tongs; never touch with fingers</td><td>Distance: the radiation intensity falls off with distance from the source, and α and β have short ranges in air</td></tr>
+          <tr><td>Keep the window pointed away from people, especially the eyes</td><td>Radiation leaves the case only through the window; the eye lens is easily damaged</td></tr>
+          <tr><td>Return the source promptly to its lead-lined box; keep the box locked and labelled with the radiation warning sign (trefoil)</td><td>Lead absorbs the radiation, including γ; the lock and label prevent accidental exposure. A wooden or plastic box is not acceptable</td></tr>
+          <tr><td>Minimise the time the source is out</td><td>Dose absorbed is proportional to exposure time</td></tr>
+          <tr><td>Wash hands after the experiment; never eat or drink in the laboratory</td><td>Removes any contamination; prevents radioactive material entering the body, where α is most damaging</td></tr>
         </tbody>
       </table>
+      <div class="def">
+        <span class="tag">Inside vs outside the body</span>
+        <p><b>Outside</b> the body γ is the most dangerous: it penetrates to internal organs, while α is stopped by a few centimetres of air or the dead outer layer of skin. <b>Inside</b> the body (breathed in or swallowed) α is the most dangerous: it is the strongest ionizer and dumps all its energy into the surrounding cells. This reversal is why radon gas is a hazard (Section F) and why α is never used as a medical tracer (Chapter 26).</p>
+      </div>
+      <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="C">
+        <h3>Concept check</h3>
+        <p>A teacher takes a sealed γ source out of its box for a demonstration. Which of the following precautions is/are justified by the reason given?</p>
+        <ol class="stem-list">
+          <li>Hold the source with long forceps, because the intensity received falls with distance from the source.</li>
+          <li>Store the source in a thick wooden box, because wood absorbs γ rays well.</li>
+          <li>Keep the demonstration short, because the dose absorbed increases with exposure time.</li>
+        </ol>
+        <div class="choices">
+          <button type="button" data-choice="A">(1) only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="B">(2) only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="C">(1) and (3) only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="D">(1), (2) and (3)</button>
+        </div>
+        <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p>(1) and (3) are the two principles that appear in every marking scheme: distance and time. (2) fails on the reason: γ passes through wood almost unhindered; the box must be lead-lined. Answer <b>C</b>.</p>
+        </div>
+      </div>
+      <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="B">
+        <h3>Concept check</h3>
+        <p>A source emits α and γ radiation. Which type of radiation is the more dangerous (i) if the source is accidentally swallowed, and (ii) if the sealed source is held in the hand?</p>
+        <div class="choices">
+          <button type="button" data-choice="A">(i) α, (ii) α</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="B">(i) α, (ii) γ</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="C">(i) γ, (ii) α</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="D">(i) γ, (ii) γ</button>
+        </div>
+        <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p><b>B</b>. Inside the body nothing shields the tissue from the α, which is the strongest ionizer, so α does the most damage. In the hand the α is stopped by the metal case or by the skin and does nothing, while γ passes through the hand and into the body. <b>A</b> and <b>D</b> ignore the location; <b>C</b> reverses the logic.</p>
+        </div>
+      </div>
     </section>
 
     <section class="idea" id="background">
       <header>
-        <h2>Background is mostly natural</h2>
-        <span class="stamp">Fig. 25.21-25.23</span>
+        <h2><span class="sec-num">F</span>Background radiation</h2>
       </header>
-      <div class="visual pie">
-        <div class="visual play stage" id="pie-vis" data-scene="pie">
-          <canvas class="scene-canvas" width="480" height="420" aria-label="Background pie"></canvas>
-          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="art">20%</span>
-        </div>
-        <div>
-          <table class="compact">
-            <thead><tr><th>slice</th><th>share</th></tr></thead>
-            <tbody>
-              <tr><td>radon (natural)</td><td>42%</td></tr>
-              <tr><td>rock / soil</td><td>16%</td></tr>
-              <tr><td>cosmic</td><td>13%</td></tr>
-              <tr><td>food / drink</td><td>9%</td></tr>
-              <tr><td>artificial (almost all medicine)</td><td>20%</td></tr>
-            </tbody>
-          </table>
-          <table class="compact pair">
-            <thead><tr><th>What you see</th><th>Therefore</th></tr></thead>
+      <p>A detector registers ionizing radiation even with no source in the room. This <b>background radiation</b> comes mostly from natural sources and reaches everyone, everywhere, all the time. In Hong Kong a GM counter typically reads about 1 count per second from background; the average annual dose is about 2.4 mSv, which is far too low to cause any apparent harm.</p>
+      <figure class="fig">
+        <div class="visual pie">
+          <div class="visual play stage" id="pie-vis" data-scene="pie">
+            <canvas class="scene-canvas" width="480" height="420" aria-label="Background pie"></canvas>
+            <span class="hud-label" data-hud="art">20%</span>
+          </div>
+          <table class="notes">
+            <thead><tr><th>Source</th><th>Share</th></tr></thead>
             <tbody>
-              <tr><td>Natural ~80%, artificial ~20%</td><td>Most background is natural; radon is the largest slice</td></tr>
-              <tr><td>Irradiation vs contamination</td><td>Irradiation does not make an object radioactive; contamination does</td></tr>
+              <tr><td>Radon gas (natural)</td><td>42%</td></tr>
+              <tr><td>Rocks and soil (natural)</td><td>16%</td></tr>
+              <tr><td>Cosmic rays (natural)</td><td>13%</td></tr>
+              <tr><td>Food, drink and our own bodies (natural)</td><td>9%</td></tr>
+              <tr><td>Artificial, almost all medical</td><td>20%</td></tr>
             </tbody>
           </table>
         </div>
+        <figcaption><b>Fig. 25.21</b> Where background radiation comes from. About 80% is natural; radon is the largest single contributor.</figcaption>
+      </figure>
+      <table class="notes">
+        <thead><tr><th>Source</th><th>What it is and what affects it</th></tr></thead>
+        <tbody>
+          <tr><td><b>Radon</b></td><td>A colourless, odourless radioactive gas produced in the U-238 decay series in rocks and soil. It seeps out of the ground and out of granite and concrete building materials and collects in poorly ventilated rooms. Being a gas it is inhaled, and it and its daughters emit α inside the lungs. The remedy is <b>ventilation</b> (open windows), not sealing the room or wearing a dust mask.</td></tr>
+          <tr><td><b>Rocks and soil</b></td><td>Radioactive minerals (uranium, thorium, potassium-40) present since the Earth formed. Granite areas and caves have higher background than the open air above them.</td></tr>
+          <tr><td><b>Cosmic rays</b></td><td>Very energetic particles from space. The atmosphere shields us, so the dose is higher on mountains and in aircraft (airline crews receive a measurably larger dose) and lower deep inside a cave or mine.</td></tr>
+          <tr><td><b>Food, drink, the body</b></td><td>All living things contain a little potassium-40 and carbon-14; so does sea water. Every human body is slightly radioactive.</td></tr>
+          <tr><td><b>Artificial</b></td><td>Medical X-rays and radiotherapy (by far the largest artificial share), industrial sources, consumer products such as smoke detectors, fallout from past nuclear weapon tests, and any leakage from nuclear power stations. Note that leakage counts as artificial background.</td></tr>
+        </tbody>
+      </table>
+      <div class="def">
+        <span class="tag">Irradiation vs contamination</span>
+        <p>An object that has been <b>irradiated</b> has only absorbed the energy of the radiation; it does <em>not</em> become radioactive. A patient after an X-ray, luggage after a security scan, or a wrapped film that has darkened under a source are irradiated, not radioactive. An object is <b>contaminated</b> when radioactive material itself gets onto or into it; it then goes on emitting radiation until the material is removed or decays. Washing hands after handling a source removes possible contamination.</p>
       </div>
-      <!-- HKDSE 2021/33 -->

The reverse question, "why is background higher on a mountain top?", has the answer cosmic rays: less atmosphere above to absorb them.</p>

+      <ul class="points">
+        <li><b>Why α ionizes most.</b> It is slow, massive and carries two units of charge, so it spends a long time near each atom it passes and pulls strongly on the electrons. β is light, fast and singly charged; γ has no charge at all and ionizes only when it happens to be absorbed by an electron.</li>
+        <li>All three <em>can</em> ionize, γ included; the differences are in how many pairs per centimetre. Because it loses energy at every ionization, the strongest ionizer runs out of energy first: ionizing power and range go opposite ways (Section F).</li>
+        <li>Nothing is ionized in a vacuum: there are no atoms to ionize. Ionizing radiation is not only nuclear radiation; X-rays ionize too.</li>
+      </ul>

+      <p>Air is normally an insulator. Ionizing radiation gives it free charge carriers, so it conducts. Everything in this section follows from that single fact.</p>
+      <table class="notes">
+        <thead><tr><th>Change made</th><th>Effect on the current, and why</th></tr></thead>
         <tbody>
-          <tr><td>+ ions to the negative plate, − ions to the positive plate</td><td>The gas conducts; a current flows</td></tr>
-          <tr><td>A charged sphere of either sign nearby</td><td>It can discharge, because ion-pairs exist</td></tr>
+          <tr><td>Replace the α source by a β source of similar activity</td><td>Current falls: β makes far fewer ion-pairs per centimetre.</td></tr>
+          <tr><td>Move the α source slowly away from the plates</td><td>Current falls to zero within a few centimetres: α has a range of only about 5 cm in air, so beyond that no air between the plates is ionized.</td></tr>
+          <tr><td>Pump the air out of the space between the plates</td><td>Current falls to zero. There is nothing to ionize, so there are no charge carriers, even though the α now travels further.</td></tr>
+          <tr><td>Blow smoke between the plates</td><td>Current falls: smoke particles capture the ions, making heavy, slow carriers. This is how an ionization smoke detector triggers its alarm (Chapter 26).</td></tr>
+          <tr><td>Bring the source near a charged metal sphere or a charged electroscope</td><td>The sphere discharges and the leaf falls, whatever the sign of its charge: ions of the opposite sign are drawn to it and neutralise it.</td></tr>
         </tbody>
       </table>

+          <canvas class="scene-canvas" width="960" height="420" aria-label="Animated cloud-chamber tracks"></canvas>
