+      </figure>
       <div class="row">
-        <button type="button" data-track="alpha" aria-pressed="true">α thick straight</button>
-        <button type="button" data-track="beta" aria-pressed="false">β thin irregular</button>
-        <button type="button" data-track="gamma" aria-pressed="false">γ faint / scattered</button>
+        <button type="button" data-track="alpha" aria-pressed="true">α tracks</button>
+        <button type="button" data-track="beta" aria-pressed="false">β tracks</button>
+        <button type="button" data-track="gamma" aria-pressed="false">γ tracks</button>
+      </div>
+      <div class="def">
+        <span class="tag">How the chamber works</span>
+        <p>Dry ice cools the base of the chamber. A felt ring soaked in alcohol near the top fills the chamber with alcohol vapour, which becomes <b>supersaturated</b> in the cold layer near the base. Radiation passing through ionizes the air along its path, and the vapour condenses on the ions into droplets. Lit from the side, the line of droplets shows up as a white track. Because tracks appear at random places and at random moments, the chamber is also direct evidence that decay is random.</p>
       </div>
-      <table class="compact pair">
-        <thead><tr><th>What you see</th><th>Therefore</th></tr></thead>
+      <table class="notes" aria-label="Table 25.5">
+        <thead><tr><th></th><th>Appearance</th><th>Why</th></tr></thead>
         <tbody>
-          <tr><td>Droplets condense on ions</td><td>Evacuate the vapour → no tracks</td></tr>
-          <tr><td>Massive / strongly ionizing</td><td>Straighter / thicker tracks</td></tr>
+          <tr><td><b>α</b></td><td>Thick, straight, short (a few cm) and all about the same length</td><td>Thick because it is a strong ionizer (many droplets per cm). Straight because it is massive and is not knocked off course by collisions with air molecules. Short because it spends its energy quickly. Equal lengths because the α particles from one source are emitted with the same energy.</td></tr>
+          <tr><td><b>β</b></td><td>Thin, twisted, longer</td><td>Thin because it ionizes weakly. Twisted because it is light and is deflected in every collision.</td></tr>
+          <tr><td><b>γ</b></td><td>Scattered, faint, short wisps, hardly observable</td><td>γ itself leaves almost no ions; the few visible tracks are made by electrons it knocks out of atoms.</td></tr>
         </tbody>
       </table>
+      <ul class="points">
+        <li>The cloud chamber is the only common detector that can tell the three radiations apart on its own. Film and a GM counter show that radiation is present and how much; they cannot say which kind without absorbers or a field.</li>
+        <li>Background radiation also makes tracks: a chamber with no source still shows occasional tracks.</li>
+        <li>In a magnetic field the tracks curve; the direction of curvature gives the sign of the charge and the radius gives the momentum.</li>
+        <li>In a chamber filled with helium, an α track sometimes forks at a right angle: the α made an oblique elastic collision with a helium nucleus of equal mass, and equal masses separate at 90°. This is evidence that the α particle <em>is</em> a helium nucleus.</li>
+      </ul>
+      <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="C">
+        <h3>Concept check</h3>
+        <p>α tracks in a cloud chamber are thick and straight. Which of the following properties of α particles explain this?</p>
+        <ol class="stem-list">
+          <li>They are heavy.</li>
+          <li>They have strong ionizing power.</li>
+          <li>They have weak penetrating power.</li>
+        </ol>
+        <div class="choices">
+          <button type="button" data-choice="A">(1) only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="B">(2) only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="C">(1) and (2) only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="D">(1), (2) and (3)</button>
+        </div>
+        <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p>Thick comes from (2): many ion-pairs per cm means many droplets. Straight comes from (1): a heavy particle is not deflected by the light air molecules it hits. (3) explains why the track is <em>short</em>, not why it is thick or straight, so it is not a reason for the property asked. Answer <b>C</b>.</p>
+        </div>
+      </div>
       <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="B">
         <h3>Concept check</h3>
-        <p>A cloud chamber has no supersaturated vapour. What follows?</p>
+        <p>Which of the following detectors, used on its own, can distinguish α, β and γ radiation?</p>
+        <ol class="stem-list">
+          <li>photographic film</li>
+          <li>cloud chamber</li>
+          <li>GM counter</li>
+        </ol>
         <div class="choices">
-          <button type="button" data-choice="A">Tracks are the particles themselves glowing.</button>
-          <button type="button" data-choice="B">No supersaturated vapour → no droplets → no visible tracks.</button>
-          <button type="button" data-choice="C">γ would still draw thick lines.</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="A">(1) only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="B">(2) only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="C">(2) and (3) only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="D">(1), (2) and (3)</button>
         </div>
         <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p>Only the cloud chamber shows a different track for each radiation. Film darkens and a GM tube counts whatever ionizing radiation reaches them; to identify the type you must add absorbers (Section G) or a field (Section H). Answer <b>B</b>.</p>
+        </div>
       </div>
     </section>
 
     <section class="idea" id="badge">
       <header>
-        <h2>Film badge: absorbers on film</h2>
-        <span class="stamp">Fig. 25.29</span>
+        <h2><span class="sec-num">D</span>Photographic film and the film badge</h2>
       </header>
-      <div class="visual play stage" id="badge-vis" data-scene="badge">
-        <canvas class="scene-canvas" width="960" height="420" aria-label="Film badge with open window, aluminium, and lead"></canvas>
-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="open">open window</span>
-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="al">thin Al</span>
-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="pb">lead</span>
-      </div>
+      <figure class="fig">
+        <div class="visual play stage" id="badge-vis" data-scene="badge">
+          <canvas class="scene-canvas" width="960" height="420" aria-label="Film badge with open window, aluminium, and lead"></canvas>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="open">open window</span>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="al">thin Al</span>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="pb">lead</span>
+        </div>
+        <figcaption><b>Fig. 25.29</b> A film badge. A piece of photographic film wrapped in opaque paper sits behind three regions: an open window, a thin aluminium sheet and a lead sheet. Choose a radiation to see how the developed film looks.</figcaption>
+      </figure>
       <div class="row">
-        <button type="button" data-badge="alpha">shine α</button>
-        <button type="button" data-badge="beta">shine β</button>
-        <button type="button" data-badge="gamma">shine γ</button>
+        <button type="button" data-badge="alpha">expose to α</button>
+        <button type="button" data-badge="beta">expose to β</button>
+        <button type="button" data-badge="gamma">expose to γ</button>
       </div>
-      <table class="compact pair">
-        <thead><tr><th>What you see</th><th>Therefore</th></tr></thead>
+      <ul class="points">
+        <li><b>Film detects radiation</b> because ionizing radiation blackens photographic film just as light does. Becquerel discovered radioactivity this way in 1896. A classic demonstration: a key or coin is laid on a film wrapped in black paper and a β source is held above it; after developing, the film is dark except for a white shadow of the key. The radiation passed through the paper but not the metal. The paper envelope has been irradiated but is not radioactive.</li>
+        <li><b>Purpose of the badge.</b> Worn by radiation workers to record the total (cumulative) dose received over weeks. Darker film means a stronger source or a longer exposure. It is cheap and simple but approximate, and it can only be read after the fact.</li>
+        <li><b>The wrapper</b> keeps visible light out; otherwise the film would blacken anyway. The same wrapper stops all α particles, so <b>a film badge can never detect α</b>. This was the point of HKDSE 2015 Paper 1A Q32, which only 35% of candidates got right.</li>
+      </ul>
+      <table class="notes">
+        <thead><tr><th>Radiation reaching the badge</th><th>Developed film</th></tr></thead>
         <tbody>
-          <tr><td>Blackening of the film</td><td>≈ dose</td></tr>
-          <tr><td>Paper/plastic wrap over the film</td><td>α is never seen</td></tr>
+          <tr><td>β only</td><td>Dark under the open window only; the aluminium and lead regions stay clear (5 mm Al stops β)</td></tr>
+          <tr><td>γ only</td><td>All three regions dark; lead region a little lighter than the others</td></tr>
+          <tr><td>β and γ</td><td>Open window darkest (β + γ); aluminium region dark (γ); lead region lightest (part of the γ)</td></tr>
+          <tr><td>α only</td><td>No darkening anywhere: the wrapper stops α before it reaches the film</td></tr>
         </tbody>
       </table>
+      <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="A">
+        <h3 data-src="after HKDSE 2015 Paper 1A Q32">Concept check</h3>
+        <p>A worker's film badge is wrapped in opaque plastic. Which type of radiation can the badge <strong>not</strong> monitor?</p>
+        <div class="choices">
+          <button type="button" data-choice="A">α</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="B">β</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="C">γ</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="D">It monitors all three.</button>
+        </div>
+        <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p><b>A</b>. α is stopped by a sheet of paper, so the light-proof wrapper alone absorbs it and nothing reaches the film. β passes the wrapper and darkens the open-window region; γ darkens every region. Most candidates chose <b>C</b>, thinking γ is "too penetrating to be caught", but γ is partly absorbed by the film and blackens it.</p>
+        </div>
+      </div>
+      <div class="check" data-check="sa">
+        <h3>Write it out</h3>
+        <p>A film badge has an open window, a 5 mm aluminium sheet and a 5 mm lead sheet in front of a wrapped film. Describe the appearance of the developed film if the wearer was exposed to (i) β only, (ii) γ only. Why is the film wrapped? <span class="marks">(4 marks)</span></p>
+        <button type="button" data-reveal>Show model answer</button>
+        <div class="model" hidden>
+          <ol>
+            <li>(i) Only the region behind the open window is darkened; the aluminium and lead regions stay clear because 5 mm of aluminium (and lead) stops β.</li>
+            <li>(ii) All three regions are darkened, the lead region least, because γ passes through aluminium almost unchanged and is only partly absorbed by lead.</li>
+            <li>The wrapper stops visible light from blackening the film, so any darkening is due to the ionizing radiation alone.</li>
+          </ol>
+          <p class="note">Pattern of darkening (1 mark each case), the absorber reason (1), the wrapper reason (1).</p>
+        </div>
+      </div>
     </section>
 
     <section class="idea" id="gm">
       <header>
-        <h2>GM tube: argon ion-pairs → pulses</h2>
-        <span class="stamp">Fig. 25.30</span>
+        <h2><span class="sec-num">E</span>The Geiger-Müller counter and count rate</h2>
       </header>
-      <div class="visual play stage" id="gm-vis" data-autoplay data-scene="gm">
-        <canvas class="scene-canvas" width="960" height="420" aria-label="Argon atom in a GM tube loses an electron toward the anode wire"></canvas>
-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="window">thin window</span>
-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="wire">+ wire</span>
-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="case">− case · argon</span>
-      </div>
-      <div class="row">
-        <span class="meter" id="gm-rate">0</span>
-        <span>count s⁻¹</span>
-        <span>corrected: <span class="meter" id="gm-corr">0</span></span>
+      <figure class="fig">
+        <div class="visual play stage" id="gm-vis" data-autoplay data-scene="gm">
+          <canvas class="scene-canvas" width="960" height="420" aria-label="Argon atom in a GM tube loses an electron toward the anode wire"></canvas>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="window">thin window</span>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="wire">+ wire</span>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="case">− case · argon</span>
+        </div>
+        <figcaption><b>Fig. 25.30</b> Inside a GM tube. Radiation enters through the thin mica window and ionizes an argon atom; the electron is pulled to the central wire and the positive ion to the case, giving one pulse of current.</figcaption>
+      </figure>
+      <div class="worked">
+        <span class="tag">How a GM counter works, step by step</span>
+        <ol class="steps">
+          <li>The tube contains argon at low pressure. A high voltage is kept between the central wire (positive, the anode) and the metal case (negative, the cathode). One end is a thin mica window.</li>
+          <li>Radiation enters through the window and ionizes argon atoms, producing ion-pairs.</li>
+          <li>The electrons are accelerated to the wire and the positive ions to the case; on the way they ionize more atoms, so a brief pulse of current flows through the tube.</li>
+          <li>Each pulse is counted by the attached scaler (total count) or ratemeter (counts per second or per minute, the <b>count rate</b>).</li>
+        </ol>
       </div>
-      <p class="caption" id="gm-bg"></p>
-      <div class="row">
-        <button type="button" id="gm-bg-btn">background only</button>
-        <button type="button" data-gm-src="40">β / γ nearby</button>
-        <button type="button" data-gm-src="20" data-need-grid="off">α (needs grid off)</button>
-        <button type="button" id="gm-grid" aria-pressed="true">plastic grid on (blocks α)</button>
+      <figure class="fig sim">
+        <div class="row">
+          <span class="row-label">Simulated ratemeter</span>
+          <span class="readout" id="gm-rate">0</span>
+          <span>count s⁻¹</span>
+          <span>corrected: <span class="readout" id="gm-corr">0</span> count s⁻¹</span>
+        </div>
+        <p class="caption" id="gm-bg"></p>
+        <div class="row">
+          <button type="button" id="gm-bg-btn">no source (background only)</button>
+          <button type="button" data-gm-src="40">β or γ source nearby</button>
+          <button type="button" data-gm-src="20" data-need-grid="off">α source nearby</button>
+          <button type="button" id="gm-grid" aria-pressed="true">plastic grid on (blocks α)</button>
+        </div>
+        <figcaption>A simulated reading. Notice that it fluctuates from second to second even with no source, and that an α source registers only when the protective plastic grid over the window is removed.</figcaption>
+      </figure>
+      <div class="eq">\[ \text{corrected count rate} = \text{recorded count rate} - \text{background count rate} \]</div>
+      <ul class="points">
+        <li><b>Always measure the background first</b>, with the source locked away, because every reading includes it. Decay is random, so readings fluctuate; take several readings and average them.</li>
+        <li>A GM counter <b>never reads zero</b>: background is always present. In Hong Kong a typical background is about 1 count per second (30 to 60 counts per minute).</li>
+        <li>Because it counts ion-pairs, the tube detects α and β more efficiently than γ, which usually passes straight through without ionizing anything. To detect α the plastic grid must be removed and the source placed within about 1.5 cm of the window; β and γ sources are placed about 5 cm away.</li>
+        <li>A GM counter is faster and more precise than film, but on its own it cannot tell which type of radiation it is counting.</li>
+        <li>The corrected count rate is proportional to the activity of the source (Chapter 26) but much smaller than it: the source radiates in all directions and the tube catches only the small fraction that enters its window, and some of that is absorbed on the way.</li>
+      </ul>
+      <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="D">
+        <h3>Concept check</h3>
+        <p>With no radioactive source in the laboratory, a GM counter gives readings of 46, 58, 41, 55 and 50 counts per 100 s in five successive trials. Which of the following statements is/are correct?</p>
+        <ol class="stem-list">
+          <li>The counter is faulty, because it should read zero without a source.</li>
+          <li>The readings differ because radioactive decay is a random process.</li>
+          <li>The best estimate of the background count rate is the mean, about 0.5 counts per second.</li>
+        </ol>
+        <div class="choices">
+          <button type="button" data-choice="A">(1) only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="B">(2) only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="C">(1) and (3) only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="D">(2) and (3) only</button>
+        </div>
+        <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p>(1) is wrong: background radiation from radon, rocks, cosmic rays and the body reaches the tube everywhere, so a non-zero reading is normal. (2) and (3) are the two things to write about any set of background readings: the scatter is randomness, and averaging reduces its effect. Answer <b>D</b>.</p>
+        </div>
       </div>
-      <div class="eq">\[ \text{corrected count rate} = \text{recorded} - \text{background} \]</div>
-      <table class="compact pair">
-        <thead><tr><th>What you see</th><th>Therefore</th></tr></thead>
-        <tbody>
-          <tr><td>Better counts for α, β than γ</td><td>γ ionizes too weakly for a thick pulse trail</td></tr>
-          <tr><td>Count rate fluctuates</td><td>Decay is random; always subtract background</td></tr>
-          <tr><td>Hong Kong typical background</td><td>~ \( 1 \) count \( \mathrm{s}^{-1} \)</td></tr>
-        </tbody>
-      </table>
     </section>
 
     <section class="idea" id="range">
       <header>
-        <h2>Range in air vs penetrating stack</h2>
-        <span class="stamp">Fig. 25.31-25.32</span>
+        <h2><span class="sec-num">F</span>Range and penetrating power</h2>
       </header>
-      <div class="visual play stage" id="abs-vis" data-autoplay data-scene="absorbers">
-        <canvas class="scene-canvas" width="960" height="420" aria-label="Paper, aluminium and lead slabs in front of a GM tube"></canvas>
-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="source">source</span>
-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="paper">paper</span>
-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="al">5 mm Al</span>
-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="pb">25 mm Pb</span>
-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="gm">GM</span>
-      </div>
+      <figure class="fig">
+        <div class="visual play stage" id="abs-vis" data-autoplay data-scene="absorbers">
+          <canvas class="scene-canvas" width="960" height="420" aria-label="Paper, aluminium and lead slabs in front of a GM tube"></canvas>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="source">source</span>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="paper">paper</span>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="al">5 mm Al</span>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="pb">25 mm Pb</span>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="gm">GM</span>
+        </div>
+        <figcaption><b>Fig. 25.31, 25.32</b> Absorbers between a source and a GM tube. Choose a source and insert absorbers; the simulated count rate uses the numbers of textbook Example 25.6 (background about 61 counts per minute).</figcaption>
+      </figure>
       <div class="row">
-        <span>Unknown:</span>
-        <button type="button" data-src="bg">β + γ</button>
-        <button type="button" data-src="ag">α + γ</button>
-        <button type="button" data-src="abg">α + β + γ</button>
+        <span class="row-label">Unknown source</span>
+        <button type="button" data-abs-src="bg">β + γ</button>
+        <button type="button" data-abs-src="ag">α + γ</button>
+        <button type="button" data-abs-src="abg">α + β + γ</button>
       </div>
       <div class="row">
+        <span class="row-label">Absorbers in place</span>
         <button type="button" id="abs-paper" aria-pressed="false">paper</button>
         <button type="button" id="abs-al" aria-pressed="false">5 mm Al</button>
         <button type="button" id="abs-pb" aria-pressed="false">25 mm Pb</button>
-        <span class="meter" id="abs-rate">0 cpm</span>
+        <span class="readout" id="abs-rate">0 cpm</span>
       </div>
       <p class="caption" id="abs-note"></p>
-      <table class="compact pair">
-        <thead><tr><th>What you see</th><th>Therefore</th></tr></thead>
+      <div class="def">
+        <span class="tag">Definitions</span>
+        <p>The <b>range</b> of a radiation in a material is the distance it travels before it has lost all its energy and stops. <b>Penetrating power</b> is the ability to pass through material. A strong ionizer loses energy quickly, so strong ionizing power means short range and weak penetrating power: α &lt; β &lt; γ in penetrating power, the reverse of Table 25.4.</p>
+      </div>
+      <table class="notes" aria-label="Table 25.6">
+        <thead><tr><th></th><th>Range in air</th><th>Stopped by</th><th>Penetrating power</th></tr></thead>
         <tbody>
-          <tr><td>In air (not to scale): α ~5 cm, β ~5 m, γ ~100 m</td><td>Range and ionizing power go opposite ways</td></tr>
-          <tr><td>Paper stops α; 5 mm Al stops β; 25 mm Pb halves γ</td><td>A drop at Al is not "β only" until α and γ are checked</td></tr>
+          <tr><td><b>α</b></td><td>a few cm (about 5 cm)</td><td>a sheet of paper, the skin, a few cm of air</td><td>weakest</td></tr>
+          <tr><td><b>β</b></td><td>a few m (about 5 m)</td><td>about 5 mm of aluminium; the count falls gradually as the aluminium thickens</td><td>strong</td></tr>
+          <tr><td><b>γ</b></td><td>very long (about 100 m, falling off with distance rather than stopping)</td><td>never completely stopped: 25 mm of lead roughly <em>halves</em> the intensity, and each further 25 mm halves it again</td><td>strongest</td></tr>
         </tbody>
       </table>
+      <div class="worked">
+        <span class="tag">Experiment: measuring the range of α in air</span>
+        <ol class="steps">
+          <li>With the source locked away, record the background count rate.</li>
+          <li>Remove the plastic grid from the GM tube and line up the α source with the window, a few millimetres away. Record the count rate.</li>
+          <li>Move the source away in small steps, recording the count rate each time, until the reading falls to the background value. Measure the separation with a metre rule: this is the range.</li>
+          <li>On a graph of count rate against distance the curve drops and then levels off; the level part is background and the distance at which it is reached is the range. To rule out gravity as the reason the α "stop", repeat with the apparatus turned through 90°: the result is the same.</li>
+        </ol>
+      </div>
+      <div class="worked">
+        <span class="tag">Worked example: γ through thick lead</span>
+        <p>A γ source gives 275 counts per minute at a GM tube; the background is 30 counts per minute. Estimate the reading with 100 mm of lead between source and tube, given that 25 mm of lead halves the γ intensity.</p>
+        <ol class="steps">
+          <li>Count from the source alone: \( 275 - 30 = 245 \) counts per minute.</li>
+          <li>100 mm is four thicknesses of 25 mm, so the source count is halved four times: \( 245 / 2^{4} \approx 15 \) counts per minute.</li>
+          <li>Add the background back: reading \( \approx 15 + 30 = 45 \) counts per minute. It can never fall below 30.</li>
+        </ol>
+      </div>
+      <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="C">
+        <h3>Concept check</h3>
+        <p>A GM tube is placed at increasing distances \( d \) from a source. The count rate falls steadily and from \( d = 4\ \mathrm{cm} \) onwards stays at about 28 counts per minute however far the tube is moved. Which conclusion is correct?</p>
+        <div class="choices">
+          <button type="button" data-choice="A">The source emits γ with a range of 4 cm.</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="B">The background count rate is zero and the source emits β.</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="C">The source emits α with a range of about 4 cm in air; the background is about 28 counts per minute.</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="D">The source stops emitting when the tube is more than 4 cm away.</button>
+        </div>
+        <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p><b>C</b>. A reading that levels off and stays constant at large distance is the background (a source never reads below it). The radiation from the source stops arriving at 4 cm, a few centimetres, which is the range of α in air. β would reach several metres and γ would fall off only slowly, so <b>A</b> and <b>B</b> are out. <b>D</b> confuses the source with what reaches the detector: decay is not affected by where the tube is.</p>
+        </div>
+      </div>
     </section>
 
     <section class="idea" id="identify">
       <header>
-        <h2>Identify α, β, γ</h2>
-        <span class="stamp">Absorber decision flow</span>
+        <h2><span class="sec-num">G</span>Identifying the radiation from absorber readings</h2>
       </header>
-      <p class="lede">Click a node, or step the example path. Yes / no on the edges.</p>
-      <div class="flow">
-        <div>
-          <svg id="id-flow" viewBox="0 0 520 600" role="img" aria-label="Decision graph for identifying radiation">
-            <defs>
-              <marker id="arrow" markerWidth="8" markerHeight="8" refX="6" refY="4" orient="auto">
-                <path d="M0,0 L8,4 L0,8 z" fill="#6b7380"/>
-              </marker>
-              <marker id="arrow-lit" markerWidth="8" markerHeight="8" refX="6" refY="4" orient="auto">
-                <path d="M0,0 L8,4 L0,8 z" fill="#0e5f56"/>
-              </marker>

-          <p class="eq">air 700 → paper 700 → Al 315 → Pb 190 &nbsp; (bg ≈ 61 cpm)</p>

+        <figcaption>The decision path. Step through the textbook example, or click a node to explore the other branches.</figcaption>
+      </figure>
+      <div class="worked">
+        <span class="tag">How to write the answer: observation, reasoning, conclusion</span>
+        <p>For Example 25.6, one line per absorber:</p>
+        <ol class="steps">
+          <li><b>Paper:</b> no significant change (700 → 700). Paper stops α. So the source emits no α.</li>
+          <li><b>5 mm aluminium:</b> large drop (700 → 315). Aluminium stops β (and would stop α). So the source emits β.</li>
+          <li><b>25 mm lead:</b> further drop (315 → 190) but still well above background (61). Lead only partly absorbs γ. So the source emits γ.</li>
+          <li><b>Conclusion:</b> β and γ, no α.</li>
+        </ol>
       </div>
-      <!-- HKDSE 2019/31 -->
+      <aside class="tip">
+        <span class="tag">The hard cases</span>
+        <p><b>No paper step.</b> 5 mm Al stops <em>both</em> α and β, so a drop at aluminium without a paper reading means "α or β or both", not "β". <b>Lead only.</b> A drop from air to 5 mm Pb proves nothing about which of α or β is present; a <em>further</em> drop from 5 mm to 15 mm Pb proves γ, and the background must be below the final reading. <b>Background not given.</b> If the reading keeps roughly halving as the lead thickens (say 90 → 46 from 25 to 50 mm), it has not reached a flat floor, so γ is still being absorbed and is present. <b>Proving "X only".</b> You need <em>no</em> significant drop at the other steps <em>and</em> a final reading at background. <b>Repeated readings</b> in a table (e.g. 210, 227; 224, 215) differ by randomness; a change only counts if it is well outside that scatter.</p>
+      </aside>
       <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="D">
-        <h3>Concept check</h3>
-        <p>Source emits α, β and γ. Cardboard, then 5 mm Al, then 10 mm Pb. Detectors at X, Y, Z after each slab.</p>
+        <h3 data-src="HKDSE 2019 Paper 1A Q31">Concept check</h3>
+        <p>A source emits α, β and γ radiation. A piece of cardboard, then a 5 mm aluminium sheet, then a 10 mm lead sheet are placed in a line in front of it. Detectors X, Y and Z are placed just after the cardboard, the aluminium and the lead respectively. Which statement is correct?</p>
         <div class="choices">
           <button type="button" data-choice="A">No radiation from the source is detected at Z.</button>
           <button type="button" data-choice="B">Both β and γ can be detected at Y.</button>
@@ -346,85 +554,195 @@
           <button type="button" data-choice="D">β is detected at X but not at Y or Z.</button>
         </div>
         <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p>Cardboard stops α, so α reaches nowhere (<b>C</b> wrong). β passes cardboard and reaches X, but 5 mm Al stops it, so it never reaches Y or Z: <b>D</b> is correct and <b>B</b> wrong. γ passes everything, reduced but present at Z (<b>A</b> wrong).</p>
+        </div>
       </div>
-      <!-- HKDSE 2014/32 -->
       <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="A">
+        <h3 data-src="HKDSE 2014 Paper 1A Q32">Concept check</h3>
+        <p>A source emits α and γ radiation. With no absorber the GM counter records 450 counts per minute; the background is 50 counts per minute. A piece of cardboard, a 1 mm aluminium sheet and a 2 mm lead sheet are then inserted in turn (one at a time), giving <em>corrected</em> count rates \( x \), \( y \) and \( z \). Which set of values is possible?</p>
+        <div class="choices">
+          <button type="button" data-choice="A">\( x = 300,\ y = 300,\ z = 100 \)</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="B">\( x = 300,\ y = 100,\ z = 50 \)</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="C">\( x = 100,\ y = 100,\ z = 0 \)</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="D">\( x = 100,\ y = 50,\ z = 50 \)</button>
+        </div>
+        <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p>Corrected rate with no absorber is \( 450 - 50 = 400 \), all from α and γ. Cardboard removes all the α and none of the γ; 1 mm Al does the same, so \( x = y \) (the γ part). 2 mm Pb absorbs some γ, so \( z \) is smaller than \( y \) but not zero. Only <b>A</b> fits. <b>B</b> has aluminium removing something extra, which would need β. <b>C</b> has lead stopping γ completely. <b>D</b> has aluminium and lead doing different things to what is left after the cardboard, but there is no β to remove.</p>
+        </div>
+      </div>
+      <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="C">
         <h3>Concept check</h3>
-        <p>α + γ source. Recorded 450 cpm, background 50. Cardboard x, 1 mm Al y, 2 mm Pb z.</p>
+        <p>Count rates (per second) measured with absorbers between a source and a GM tube: none 400; paper 398; 5 mm Al 180; 25 mm Pb 90; 50 mm Pb 46. The background was not measured. The source emits</p>
         <div class="choices">
-          <button type="button" data-choice="A">x = 300, y = 300, z = 100</button>
-          <button type="button" data-choice="B">x = 300, y = 100, z = 50</button>
-          <button type="button" data-choice="C">x = 100, y = 100, z = 0</button>
-          <button type="button" data-choice="D">x = 100, y = 50, z = 50</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="A">β only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="B">γ only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="C">β and γ only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="D">α, β and γ</button>
         </div>
         <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p>Paper: no drop, so no α (<b>D</b> out). Aluminium: a large drop, so β is present (<b>B</b> out). Lead: the count halves again between 25 mm and 50 mm, so it is not sitting at a flat background floor; something is still being attenuated, which must be γ (<b>A</b> out). Answer <b>C</b>. This is how to reason when the background is not given: a reading that keeps changing with thickness is not background.</p>
+        </div>
+      </div>
+      <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="B">
+        <h3>Concept check</h3>
+        <p>With a source in front of a GM tube the readings are: no absorber 504; 5 mm lead 261; 15 mm lead 157 (counts per minute). No other absorbers were used. Which of the following can be concluded?</p>
+        <ol class="stem-list">
+          <li>The source emits β.</li>
+          <li>The source emits γ.</li>
+          <li>The background count rate is about 157 counts per minute.</li>
+        </ol>
+        <div class="choices">
+          <button type="button" data-choice="A">(1) only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="B">(2) only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="C">(1) and (2) only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="D">(2) and (3) only</button>
+        </div>
+        <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p>Only (2). The drop from 261 to 157 as the lead thickens shows γ is present. The first drop (504 → 261) could be α, β or both being stopped by the 5 mm lead, so (1) is not proved. (3) is wrong because 15 mm of lead does not stop γ; the background is somewhere <em>below</em> 157. Answer <b>B</b>.</p>
+        </div>
+      </div>
+      <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="B">
+        <h3 data-src="after HKDSE 2018 Paper 1B Q10">Concept check</h3>
+        <p>A spark counter sparks when a radium-226 source is held close to its wire gauze. A student claims that the sparks are caused by the α particles rather than by the β or γ that the source also emits. Which test best verifies this claim?</p>
+        <div class="choices">
+          <button type="button" data-choice="A">Photograph the tracks in a cloud chamber to show that α, β and γ are all present.</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="B">Insert a sheet of paper between the source and the gauze, or move the source more than about 5 cm away, and check that the sparking stops.</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="C">Bring a bar magnet close to the source and see whether the sparks change.</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="D">Replace the high-voltage supply with a lower voltage.</button>
+        </div>
+        <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p><b>B</b>: a test that removes only the α (paper, or distance beyond its range) and nothing else. If the sparks stop, the α were responsible. <b>A</b> shows what the source emits but not what causes the sparks; the examiners' report noted that many candidates gave this answer. <b>C</b> barely affects α and is inconclusive. <b>D</b> changes the detector, not the radiation.</p>
+        </div>
+      </div>
+      <div class="check" data-check="sa">
+        <h3>Write it out</h3>
+        <p>A source is placed 10 cm from a GM tube. Two readings are taken with each absorber (counts per minute): no absorber 210, 227; paper 224, 215; 5 mm Al 45, 47; 10 mm Pb 48, 42. The background is about 45 counts per minute. Show that the source emits β only. <span class="marks">(6 marks)</span></p>
+        <button type="button" data-reveal>Show model answer</button>
+        <div class="model" hidden>
+          <ol>
+            <li><b>Paper.</b> Observation: no significant change (about 220 both times; the two repeat readings differ by about as much). Reasoning: paper stops α. Conclusion: no α is emitted.</li>
+            <li><b>5 mm aluminium.</b> Observation: large drop to about 46. Reasoning: aluminium stops β. Conclusion: β is emitted.</li>
+            <li><b>10 mm lead.</b> Observation: no further drop; the reading (about 45) equals the background. Reasoning: lead would reduce γ but there is nothing above background left to reduce. Conclusion: no γ is emitted.</li>
+          </ol>
+          <p class="note">Each absorber earns an observation mark and a reasoning-plus-conclusion mark. The "β only" claim needs all three lines: what paper did not do, what aluminium did, and that lead found nothing left.</p>
+        </div>
       </div>

-          <tr><td>β mass ≈ 1/7200 of α, so β bends much more</td><td>|q| is smaller but m is far smaller, so deflection is larger</td></tr>
-          <tr><td>Fleming left-hand: β travels opposite to conventional I</td><td>α and β curve opposite ways; γ goes straight</td></tr>
-          <tr><td>α stops in a few cm of air</td><td>Do E/B in vacuum if you need the α path</td></tr>
+          <tr><td><b>α</b> (+2, heavy)</td><td>Force along \( E \), towards the negative plate; small deflection</td><td>Fleming's left-hand rule with the current in the direction of motion; small deflection</td></tr>
+          <tr><td><b>β</b> (−1, light)</td><td>Force against \( E \), towards the positive plate; large deflection</td><td>Fleming's left-hand rule with the current <em>opposite</em> to the motion (negative charge); large deflection, opposite side to α</td></tr>
+          <tr><td><b>γ</b> (no charge)</td><td colspan="2">No force, no deflection</td></tr>

-      <!-- HKDSE 2017/31 -->
-      <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="A">
+      <ul class="points">
+        <li><b>Why β bends more.</b> Not because of a larger force: the force on an α is twice as large (charge 2e against e). The β is deflected far more because its mass is about 1/7200 of the α's, so the same order of force gives it a far greater acceleration. The degree of deflection depends on mass, charge magnitude and speed; the <em>sign</em> of the charge only fixes the side.</li>
+        <li><b>The collimator</b> (two lead blocks with a narrow slit) turns the radiation leaving the source in all directions into a fine beam travelling in one direction, so that a deflection can be seen. If film is the detector, the apparatus must be in the dark.</li>
+        <li><b>β spreads out.</b> β particles leave a source with a range of speeds, so they are deflected by different amounts and fan out over a region instead of hitting one spot. A GM tube at one point therefore catches only some of them, and the total β count with the field on is less than with it off. α particles from one source all have nearly the same speed, so they stay together.</li>
+        <li><b>In air</b> the α is absorbed within a few centimetres and is usually missing from the picture; to see the α path the apparatus must be evacuated.</li>
+        <li><b>Reading a detector scan.</b> Place γ at the undeflected position, β on the side the rule predicts, α (if any) on the other side. Two positions with equal small counts are both reading background. A missing peak does not prove a radiation is absent if it was deflected off the line of the scan or absorbed before arriving.</li>
+      </ul>
+      <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="B">
         <h3>Concept check</h3>
-        <p>Uniform E in vacuum. Pick the path pair: α toward the negative plate, β the other way and bent more.</p>
+        <p>In a uniform electric field β particles are deflected much more than α particles of similar kinetic energy. This is mainly because β particles</p>
         <div class="choices">
-          <button type="button" data-choice="A">α gentle curve to − ; β sharp curve to +</button>
-          <button type="button" data-choice="B">both curve the same way</button>
-          <button type="button" data-choice="C">β gentle, α sharp (mass the wrong way)</button>
-          <button type="button" data-choice="D">γ curves with α</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="A">experience a larger electric force.</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="B">have a much smaller mass.</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="C">carry more charge.</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="D">move more slowly.</button>
         </div>
         <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p><b>B</b>. The force on a β (charge e) is half that on an α (charge 2e), so <b>A</b> and <b>C</b> are wrong; what makes the difference is the mass, about 7200 times smaller, giving a far larger acceleration. <b>D</b> is wrong twice: β is faster, and a faster particle spends less time in the field, which would reduce the deflection.</p>
+        </div>
       </div>
-      <!-- HKDSE 2017/32 -->
       <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="C">
-        <h3>Concept check</h3>
-        <p>Which statement about β and γ is correct?</p>
+        <h3 data-src="HKDSE 2017 Paper 1A Q32">Concept check</h3>
+        <p>Which of the following statements about β and γ radiation is correct?</p>
         <div class="choices">
-          <button type="button" data-choice="A">Only β can ionize air.</button>
-          <button type="button" data-choice="B">Only γ can travel through vacuum.</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="A">Only β radiation can ionize air.</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="B">Only γ radiation can travel through a vacuum.</button>
           <button type="button" data-choice="C">Both can be detected by a photographic film.</button>
           <button type="button" data-choice="D">Both carry charge.</button>
         </div>
         <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p><b>C</b>: both are ionizing and both blacken film. <b>A</b>: γ ionizes too, weakly. <b>B</b>: both cross a vacuum (nothing is needed to carry either). <b>D</b>: γ has no charge, which is exactly why fields do not deflect it.</p>
+        </div>
+      </div>
+      <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="B">
+        <h3>Concept check</h3>
+        <p>A collimated beam from a source enters a uniform magnetic field directed into the page. Beyond the field a GM tube on the original line of the beam reads 95 counts per minute; a second GM tube placed below the line reads 520 counts per minute. Which deduction <strong>must</strong> be correct?</p>
+        <div class="choices">
+          <button type="button" data-choice="A">The source emits γ radiation.</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="B">The source emits β radiation.</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="C">The source does not emit α radiation.</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="D">The background count rate is 95 counts per minute.</button>
+        </div>
+        <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p><b>B</b>. For a beam moving to the right in a field into the page, Fleming's rule sends a positive charge upwards and a negative charge downwards; a large count below the line must be β. <b>A</b>: 95 on the line could be γ, or could be entirely background, so γ is not proved. <b>C</b>: no tube was placed above the line, and α would in any case be absorbed by the air before arriving, so nothing can be said about α. <b>D</b>: 95 is an upper limit on the background (it may include some γ), not its value.</p>
+        </div>
+      </div>
+      <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="A">
+        <h3>Concept check</h3>
+        <p>A GM tube on the straight-through line from a source reads 184 counts per minute with no field and 82 counts per minute when a strong magnetic field is switched on across the beam. A tube far from the source reads 42 counts per minute. Estimate the β and γ count rates reaching the tube on the line, assuming any α is absorbed by the air.</p>
+        <div class="choices">
+          <button type="button" data-choice="A">β ≈ 102, γ ≈ 40 counts per minute</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="B">β ≈ 82, γ ≈ 42 counts per minute</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="C">β ≈ 142, γ ≈ 82 counts per minute</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="D">β ≈ 184, γ ≈ 82 counts per minute</button>
+        </div>
+        <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p>The field removes the β from the line and leaves the γ and background: β ≈ 184 − 82 = 102. The far tube reads background only, so γ ≈ 82 − 42 = 40. Answer <b>A</b>. The other options forget to subtract either the field-on reading or the background.</p>
+        </div>
       </div>
     </section>

X-rays are ionizing EM waves of very short wavelength; they are not nuclear radiation and a radioactive source never emits them.</figcaption>

-        <thead>

-        </thead>
+      <ul class="points">
+        <li>Balance top and bottom separately: total \( A \) and total \( Z \) are the same on both sides. Emitted particles go on the right.</li>
+        <li>Every member of a decay series has \( A \) differing from the parent's by a multiple of 4; the end product is the lead isotope that satisfies this.</li>
+        <li>Isotopes: same \( Z \), different \( A \) (different \( N \)). Same chemistry, different mass. Not "same \( A \)".</li>
+      </ul>

-    <section class="idea">
+    <section class="idea" id="traps">
       <header>
-        <h2>Three traps</h2>
-        <span class="stamp">Common mistakes</span>
+        <h2><span class="sec-num">5</span>Traps that cost marks</h2>
       </header>
       <div class="trap-grid">
         <div class="trap">
-          <h3>1. 5 mm Al drop</h3>
-          <p>does not prove "β only" until you eliminate α and γ.</p>
+          <h3>A drop at 5 mm Al is not "β only"</h3>
+          <p>Aluminium stops α as well as β. You need the paper step to rule out α and the lead step (a reading at background) to rule out γ.</p>
+        </div>
+        <div class="trap">
+          <h3>Lead halves γ; it never zeroes it</h3>
+          <p>Each 25 mm of lead halves the γ. A reading can never fall below background, and a reading that keeps changing with thickness is not background.</p>
         </div>
         <div class="trap">
-          <h3>2. β electron</h3>
-          <p>from the nucleus, not from an orbit.</p>
+          <h3>β bends more because of mass, not force</h3>
+          <p>The force on an α is twice as large. The β is deflected more because it is about 7200 times lighter.</p>
         </div>
         <div class="trap">
-          <h3>3. Half-life graphs</h3>
-          <p>wait for the next chapter: activity, sievert, C-14 dating, nuclear energy.</p>
+          <h3>The β electron comes from the nucleus</h3>
+          <p>A neutron becomes a proton and an electron. It is not an orbital electron, and no neutron is emitted.</p>
+        </div>
+        <div class="trap">
+          <h3>Ions do the work, not the α</h3>
+          <p>A charged object near an α source is neutralised by ions of the opposite sign drawn out of the ionized air. Moving the source a few centimetres away ends the effect.</p>
+        </div>
+        <div class="trap">
+          <h3>X-rays are not nuclear radiation</h3>
+          <p>Undeflected radiation from a radioactive source is γ. X-rays and γ differ only in origin; both are ionizing EM waves.</p>
+        </div>
+        <div class="trap">
+          <h3>Irradiated is not contaminated</h3>
+          <p>An X-rayed patient or a wrapped film under a source has absorbed energy and is not radioactive. Only radioactive material on or in an object makes it radioactive.</p>
+        </div>
+        <div class="trap">
+          <h3>"α particle", not "α decay"</h3>
+          <p>When asked to state the kind of radiation, name the particle. A radium source also gives β and γ because its daughters are radioactive, not because it is impure.</p>
         </div>
       </div>
     </section>

Of the two charged radiations the light β curves sharply, so R = β and P = α: <b>B</b>. The two curves are on opposite sides because the charges have opposite signs.</p>

+          <li>R has one more proton than Q.</li>
+          <li>The mass numbers of Q and R are equal.</li>
+        </ol>
+        <div class="choices">

+        </div>
+        <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>

   <main>
     <p class="kicker">26.1</p>
     <h1>Rate of radioactive decay</h1>
+    <p class="lede">Decay is random for one nucleus and perfectly predictable for a large sample. This section builds the two working tools, half-life and activity, shows how to read them from real (background-contaminated) data, and then, as extension, the decay constant and the exponential law that let you solve for any time, not only whole half-lives.</p>
 
     <section class="lo-block" aria-labelledby="lo-heading">
       <h2 id="lo-heading">Learning objectives</h2>

       </ul>
     </section>

     <section class="idea" id="random">
       <header>
-        <h2>Decay is random, a sample is not chaos</h2>
-        <span class="stamp">Fig. 26.1–26.3</span>
+        <h2><span class="sec-num">A</span>Decay is random, but a sample is predictable</h2>
       </header>
-      <div class="visual play stage" id="decay-vis" data-autoplay data-scene="dice">
-        <canvas class="scene-canvas" width="960" height="420" aria-label="One hundred nuclei. Each throw, about one in six decay and are removed."></canvas>
-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="live">100 undecayed</span>

+      <figure class="fig">
+        <div class="visual play stage" id="decay-vis" data-autoplay data-scene="dice">
+          <canvas class="scene-canvas" width="960" height="420" aria-label="One hundred nuclei. Each throw, about one in six decay and are removed."></canvas>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="live">100 undecayed</span>
+        </div>
+        <figcaption><b>Fig. 26.1 to 26.3</b> A dice model. Each nucleus is a die; on every throw each surviving die has the same 1-in-6 chance of "decaying" and is removed. Nobody can say which dice will go on the next throw, yet about one sixth of the survivors always do.</figcaption>
+      </figure>
       <div class="row">
         <button type="button" data-replay="decay-vis">Replay</button>
       </div>
-      <table class="compact pair">
-        <thead><tr><th>What you see</th><th>Therefore</th></tr></thead>
-        <tbody>
-          <tr><td>You cannot name which nucleus decays on the next throw</td><td>Radioactive decay is a random process</td></tr>
-          <tr><td>Each nucleus has the same chance (here 1 in 6) each throw</td><td>A large sample still has a predictable pattern</td></tr>
-          <tr><td>Fewer live nuclei remain, so fewer decay on the next throw</td><td>The rate falls as N falls</td></tr>
-        </tbody>
-      </table>
+      <div class="def">
+        <span class="tag">Random and spontaneous</span>
+        <p>Radioactive decay is <b>random</b>: it is impossible to predict when a particular nucleus will decay, and every undecayed nucleus of a given nuclide has the same chance of decaying in the next second, however old it is. It is <b>spontaneous</b>: it happens on its own and nothing external (temperature, pressure, chemical bonding, an electric field) speeds it up or slows it down.</p>
+      </div>
+      <ul class="points">
+        <li>Because every nucleus has the same chance per unit time, the <em>number</em> decaying per second is proportional to the number of undecayed nuclei present. Fewer survivors, fewer decays. That is why the rate falls as the sample decays.</li>
+        <li>With a large number of nuclei (a microgram of anything contains about \( 10^{15} \) atoms) the statistical average is so reliable that the decay of a sample follows a smooth, predictable curve.</li>
+        <li>With very few nuclei the average is not reliable. If a million boxes each hold two nuclei and you wait one half-life, some boxes will have 0 left, some 1, some 2; only the <em>average</em> is 1. "Half of two nuclei decays after one half-life" is not a meaningful statement.</li>
+        <li>Evidence of randomness: repeated equal-time counts of the same source (or of background) are never identical, and cloud-chamber tracks appear at random places and moments.</li>
+      </ul>
       <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="D">
         <h3>Concept check</h3>
-        <p>Radioactive decay is random in nature. This means that</p>
+        <p>Radioactive decay is described as random. This means that</p>
         <div class="choices">
           <button type="button" data-choice="A">the activity of a sample depends on the number of undecayed nuclei.</button>
           <button type="button" data-choice="B">the rate of decay increases with temperature.</button>
-          <button type="button" data-choice="C">the type of radiation emitted by the sample is unknown.</button>
-          <button type="button" data-choice="D">we can never tell which nucleus in the sample will decay first.</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="C">the type of radiation emitted by the sample cannot be known in advance.</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="D">we can never tell which nucleus in the sample will decay next, or when a given nucleus will decay.</button>
         </div>
         <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p><b>D</b> is the definition. <b>A</b> is true but is a <em>consequence</em> of every nucleus having the same chance, not what "random" means. <b>B</b> is false: decay is spontaneous and temperature has no effect. <b>C</b> is false: a given nuclide always emits the same radiation; what is unknown is the timing.</p>
+        </div>
       </div>
       <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="D">
-        <h3>Concept check</h3>
-        <p>Four one-minute background counts are taken. Which set is the most probable?</p>
+        <h3 data-src="HKDSE 2020 Paper 1A Q30">Concept check</h3>
+        <p>Four one-minute background counts are taken in the same laboratory. Which set of readings is the most probable?</p>
         <div class="choices">
           <button type="button" data-choice="A">5, 62, 8, 69</button>
           <button type="button" data-choice="B">40, 40, 40, 40</button>
@@ -81,43 +88,81 @@
           <button type="button" data-choice="D">29, 26, 31, 35</button>
         </div>
         <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p>Background is roughly constant on average but random in detail, so consecutive counts should scatter <em>a little</em> about a mean: <b>D</b>. <b>B</b> shows no randomness at all; <b>A</b> swings far too wildly for a steady source; <b>C</b> falls steadily, which would mean a decaying source, not background.</p>
+        </div>
+      </div>
+      <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="C">
+        <h3>Concept check</h3>
+        <p>A sample contains a very large number of nuclei of one radionuclide. Which of the following statements is/are correct?</p>
+        <ol class="stem-list">
+          <li>The nuclei that have survived longest are the most likely to decay next.</li>
+          <li>The number decaying per second falls as time goes on.</li>
+          <li>Cooling the sample would slow its decay.</li>
+        </ol>
+        <div class="choices">
+          <button type="button" data-choice="A">(1) only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="B">(3) only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="C">(2) only</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="D">(1) and (2) only</button>
+        </div>
+        <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p>(1) is false: nuclei do not age; every survivor has the same chance per second. (2) is true: fewer undecayed nuclei remain, so fewer decay per second. (3) is false: decay is spontaneous and unaffected by temperature. Answer <b>C</b>.</p>
+        </div>
       </div>
     </section>
 
     <section class="idea" id="halflife">
       <header>
-        <h2>Half-life: N halves on a fixed clock</h2>
-        <span class="stamp">Fig. 26.4</span>
+        <h2><span class="sec-num">B</span>Half-life</h2>
       </header>
-      <div class="visual play stage" id="halfn-vis" data-autoplay data-scene="halfN">
-        <canvas class="scene-canvas" width="960" height="420" aria-label="Iodine-131: number of undecayed nuclei against time, with remaining and decayed stacks"></canvas>
-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="n">N</span>
-        <span class="hud-label" data-hud="t">t</span>
+      <figure class="fig">
+        <div class="visual play stage" id="halfn-vis" data-autoplay data-scene="halfN">
+          <canvas class="scene-canvas" width="960" height="420" aria-label="Iodine-131: number of undecayed nuclei against time, with remaining and decayed stacks"></canvas>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="n">N</span>
+          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="t">t</span>
+        </div>
+        <figcaption><b>Fig. 26.4</b> Iodine-131 (half-life 8 days). Starting with 40 billion nuclei, 20 billion remain at 8 days, 10 billion at 16 days, 5 billion at 24 days. The grey stack is the decayed nuclei, which have become daughter nuclei (xenon-131) and are still there: the two stacks always total 40 billion.</figcaption>
+      </figure>
+      <div class="def">
+        <span class="tag">Definition</span>
+        <p>The <b>half-life</b> \( T_{1/2} \) of a radionuclide is the time taken for the number of undecayed nuclei in a sample to fall to half its initial value. Equivalently, it is the time for the activity to fall to half. After \( n \) half-lives,</p>
+        <div class="eq">\[ N = N_0 \left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)^{n}, \qquad n = \frac{t}{T_{1/2}} \]</div>
       </div>
-      <table class="compact pair">
-        <thead><tr><th>What you see</th><th>Therefore</th></tr></thead>
-        <tbody>
-          <tr><td>After 8 d, 20 billion remain and 20 billion have decayed</td><td>Half-life of I-131 is 8 days</td></tr>
-          <tr><td>The orange + grey stack still totals 40 billion</td><td>Decayed nuclei become a daughter; they do not vanish</td></tr>
-          <tr><td>The same halving repeats at 16 d and 24 d</td><td>\( N = N_0 (1/2)^{n} \) after n half-lives</td></tr>
-        </tbody>
-      </table>
-      <div class="eq">\[ N = N_0 \left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)^{n} \]</div>
-      <table class="compact">
-        <thead><tr><th>nuclide</th><th>half-life</th></tr></thead>
+      <ul class="points">
+        <li>Half-life is a property of the nuclide, not of the sample. A gram and a microgram of iodine-131 both have \( T_{1/2} = 8 \) d. Heating, cooling, dissolving or splitting the sample does not change it.</li>
+        <li>Half-life is not "how long the source lasts". After one half-life half the nuclei are still undecayed; after two, a quarter; the number approaches zero but a sample is never "finished" at any particular moment.</li>
+        <li>The <em>decayed</em> nuclei do not disappear. They become the daughter nuclide, so the total number of nuclei (and, very nearly, the mass) stays constant while the sample becomes progressively more daughter and less parent.</li>
+        <li>Half-lives range over more than twenty powers of ten:</li>
+      </ul>
+      <table class="notes" aria-label="Table 26.3">
+        <thead><tr><th>Radionuclide</th><th>Half-life</th><th>Radionuclide</th><th>Half-life</th></tr></thead>
         <tbody>
-          <tr><td>iodine-131</td><td>8 d</td></tr>
-          <tr><td>carbon-14</td><td>5700 y</td></tr>
-          <tr><td>uranium-238</td><td>\( 4.47 \times 10^{9} \) y</td></tr>
+          <tr><td>polonium-215</td><td>0.0018 s</td><td>iodine-131</td><td>8 d</td></tr>
+          <tr><td>radon-220</td><td>52 s</td><td>strontium-90</td><td>28.8 y</td></tr>
+          <tr><td>bismuth-213</td><td>45.6 min</td><td>carbon-14</td><td>5730 y (often rounded to 5700 y)</td></tr>
+          <tr><td>sodium-24</td><td>15 h</td><td>uranium-238</td><td>\( 4.47 \times 10^{9} \) y</td></tr>
         </tbody>
       </table>
-      <div class="one-liners">
-        <p>Each radionuclide has a characteristic half-life. Heating or chemistry does not change it.</p>
+      <div class="worked">
+        <span class="tag">Worked example</span>
+        <p>A sample of a radionuclide with half-life 3 days has mass 100 g. (a) What mass of the nuclide remains after 12 days? (b) What mass has decayed? (c) What was the mass 6 days before the start?</p>
+        <ol class="steps">
+          <li>(a) \( n = 12/3 = 4 \) half-lives. Remaining \( = 100 \times (1/2)^4 = 6.25 \) g.</li>
+          <li>(b) Decayed \( = 100 - 6.25 = 93.75 \) g. (It is still in the sample, as daughter nuclide.)</li>
+          <li>(c) Going backwards two half-lives doubles twice: \( 100 \times 2^2 = 400 \) g.</li>
+        </ol>
+        <p class="note">Common slips: giving 6.25 g for "decayed", or 75 g (two half-lives) for "decayed in 12 days".</p>
       </div>
       <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="B">
         <h3>Concept check</h3>
-        <p>Which statements about the half-life of a source are correct?</p>
-        <p>(1) Time for the activity to fall to half. (2) Time left for the source to remain radioactive. (3) Time for the number of undecayed nuclei to drop to half.</p>
+        <p>Which of the following statements about the half-life of a radioactive source is/are correct?</p>
+        <ol class="stem-list">
+          <li>It is the time for the activity of the source to fall to half its value.</li>
+          <li>It is the time the source remains radioactive.</li>
+          <li>It is the time for the number of undecayed nuclei to fall to half its value.</li>
+        </ol>
         <div class="choices">
           <button type="button" data-choice="A">(1) only</button>
           <button type="button" data-choice="B">(1) and (3) only</button>
@@ -125,11 +170,18 @@
           <button type="button" data-choice="D">(1), (2) and (3)</button>
         </div>
         <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p>(3) is the definition and (1) follows because \( A \propto N \). (2) is the standard misconception: after one half-life the source is still radioactive with half its nuclei left. Answer <b>B</b>.</p>
+        </div>
       </div>
       <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="C">
         <h3>Concept check</h3>
-        <p>A source contains only radioactive X, which decays to stable Y. After one half-life of X:</p>
-        <p>(1) The number of X nuclei is about half. (2) The number of Y nuclei is about twice its initial value. (3) The activity of the source is about half.</p>
+        <p>A source contains only radioactive nuclide X, which decays to stable Y. After one half-life of X, which of the following is/are correct?</p>
+        <ol class="stem-list">
+          <li>The number of X nuclei is about half the initial number.</li>
+          <li>The number of Y nuclei is about twice its initial value.</li>
+          <li>The activity of the source is about half its initial value.</li>
+        </ol>
         <div class="choices">
           <button type="button" data-choice="A">(1) only</button>
           <button type="button" data-choice="B">(2) only</button>
@@ -137,60 +189,105 @@
           <button type="button" data-choice="D">(2) and (3) only</button>
         </div>
         <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p>(1) and (3) are the definition of half-life applied to \( N \) and \( A \). (2) is a trap: the source started with <em>no</em> Y, so there is no "initial value" to double. After one half-life the Y nuclei equal the X nuclei. Answer <b>C</b>.</p>
+        </div>
+      </div>
+      <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="C">
+        <h3>Concept check</h3>
+        <p>A pure sample of a radionuclide decays to a stable daughter. After 2 years the sample is 2% parent and 98% daughter by number. What was its composition after 1 year?</p>
+        <div class="choices">
+          <button type="button" data-choice="A">25% parent, 75% daughter</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="B">51% parent, 49% daughter</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="C">14% parent, 86% daughter</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="D">4% parent, 96% daughter</button>
+        </div>
+        <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p>Whatever fraction survives one year, the same fraction of <em>that</em> survives the next year. So \( f^2 = 0.02 \), \( f = \sqrt{0.02} = 0.14 \): <b>C</b>. <b>A</b> assumes the fraction falls linearly; <b>B</b> assumes half of the loss happens each year; <b>D</b> doubles instead of taking the square root.</p>
+        </div>
       </div>
     </section>
 
     <section class="idea" id="activity">
       <header>
-        <h2>Activity halves on the same clock</h2>
-        <span class="stamp">Fig. 26.5–26.6</span>
+        <h2><span class="sec-num">C</span>Activity</h2>
       </header>
-      <div class="pane-pair">
-        <div class="visual play stage" id="activityn-vis" data-autoplay data-scene="activityN">
-          <canvas class="scene-canvas" width="480" height="360" aria-label="Undecayed nuclei drop by half, so activity drops by half"></canvas>
-          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="a">A</span>
-          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="n">N</span>
-        </div>
-        <div class="visual play stage" id="activitya-vis" data-autoplay data-scene="activityA">
-          <canvas class="scene-canvas" width="480" height="360" aria-label="Activity against time for iodine-131"></canvas>
-          <span class="hud-label" data-hud="a">A</span>
+      <figure class="fig">
+        <div class="pane-pair">
+          <div class="visual play stage" id="activityn-vis" data-autoplay data-scene="activityN">
+            <canvas class="scene-canvas" width="480" height="360" aria-label="Undecayed nuclei drop by half, so activity drops by half"></canvas>
+            <span class="hud-label" data-hud="a">A</span>
+            <span class="hud-label" data-hud="n">N</span>
+          </div>
+          <div class="visual play stage" id="activitya-vis" data-autoplay data-scene="activityA">
+            <canvas class="scene-canvas" width="480" height="360" aria-label="Activity against time for iodine-131"></canvas>
+            <span class="hud-label" data-hud="a">A</span>
+          </div>
         </div>
+        <figcaption><b>Fig. 26.5 and 26.6</b> Left: fewer undecayed nuclei means fewer decays per second. Right: the activity of iodine-131 against time has exactly the same shape, and the same half-life, as the graph of \( N \).</figcaption>
+      </figure>
+      <div class="def">
+        <span class="tag">Definition</span>
+        <p>The <b>activity</b> \( A \) of a sample is the number of nuclei that decay per unit time. Unit: the <b>becquerel</b> (Bq), 1 Bq = 1 decay per second. Because every undecayed nucleus has the same chance of decaying per second, activity is proportional to the number of undecayed nuclei:</p>
+        <div class="eq">\[ A \propto N \qquad\text{so}\qquad A = A_0 \left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)^{n} \]</div>
+      </div>
+      <ul class="points">
+        <li>Activity depends on <em>two</em> things: how many undecayed nuclei there are, and the nuclide's half-life (a short half-life means each nucleus is more likely to decay per second). It does not depend on the type of radiation emitted.</li>
+        <li>Halving a sample halves \( N \) and therefore \( A \). It leaves the half-life unchanged.</li>
+        <li><b>Count rate is not activity.</b> A GM tube some distance away records far fewer counts per second than the activity in Bq: radiation is emitted in all directions and the tube intercepts only a small fraction; some is absorbed on the way; the tube does not register every particle that enters it. The corrected count rate is <em>proportional</em> to activity, so it halves on the same clock and can be used to find the half-life.</li>
+        <li>Non-integer numbers of half-lives are allowed: \( n = t / T_{1/2} \) need not be a whole number. \( A = A_0 (1/2)^{t/T_{1/2}} \) works for any \( t \).</li>
+      </ul>
+      <div class="worked">
+        <span class="tag">Worked example</span>
+        <p>Iodine-131 has a half-life of 8 days. A sample has activity 200 Bq after 30 days. What was its initial activity?</p>
+        <ol class="steps">
+          <li>\( n = 30/8 = 3.75 \) half-lives (do not round to 3 or 4).</li>
+          <li>\( A_0 = A \times 2^{n} = 200 \times 2^{3.75} = 200 \times 13.45 = 2690 \) Bq.</li>
+        </ol>
+        <p class="note">Rounding \( n \) to 4 gives 3200 Bq; to 3 gives 1600 Bq; multiplying by 3.75 gives 750 Bq. All three appear as distractors.</p>
       </div>
-      <table class="compact pair">
-        <thead><tr><th>What you see</th><th>Therefore</th></tr></thead>
-        <tbody>
-          <tr><td>Activity is the number of nuclei decaying per unit time</td><td>Unit: becquerel. 1 Bq = 1 s⁻¹</td></tr>
-          <tr><td>Fewer undecayed nuclei left</td><td>Lower activity. \( A \propto N \)</td></tr>
-          <tr><td>A halves when N halves</td><td>\( A = A_0 (1/2)^{n} \) as well</td></tr>
-        </tbody>
-      </table>
-      <div class="eq">\[ A \propto N \quad\text{so}\quad A = A_0 \left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)^{n} \]</div>
       <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="B">
-        <h3>Concept check</h3>
-        <p>A source undergoes decay such that 300 nuclides have decayed in 1 minute. Its activity is</p>
+        <h3 data-src="HKDSE 2025 Paper 1A Q31">Concept check</h3>
+        <p>A source undergoes decay such that 300 nuclei decay in 1 minute. Its activity is</p>
         <div class="choices">
           <button type="button" data-choice="A">0.2 Bq</button>
           <button type="button" data-choice="B">5 Bq</button>
           <button type="button" data-choice="C">300 Bq</button>
-          <button type="button" data-choice="D">18000 Bq</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="D">18 000 Bq</button>
         </div>
         <p class="feedback"></p>

+          <p>Activity is decays <em>per second</em>: \( 300 / 60 = 5 \) Bq, <b>B</b>. <b>C</b> forgets the unit conversion; <b>D</b> multiplies instead of dividing; <b>A</b> inverts.</p>
+        </div>
       </div>
-      <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="D">
+      <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="A">
         <h3>Concept check</h3>
-        <p>The activity of a sample is 18 MBq. What was it 3 half-lives earlier?</p>
+        <p>Half of a powdered radioactive sample is removed. For the half that remains, which of the following are halved compared with the original sample?</p>
+        <ol class="stem-list">
+          <li>the activity</li>
+          <li>the half-life</li>
+          <li>the number of undecayed nuclei</li>
+        </ol>
         <div class="choices">
-          <button type="button" data-choice="A">6 MBq</button>
-          <button type="button" data-choice="B">54 MBq</button>
-          <button type="button" data-choice="C">72 MBq</button>
-          <button type="button" data-choice="D">144 MBq</button>

         </div>
         <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p>Half the powder means half the nuclei (3), and since \( A \propto N \) half the activity (1). The half-life is a property of the nuclide and is unchanged. Answer <b>A</b>.</p>
+        </div>
       </div>
       <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="D">
         <h3>Concept check</h3>
         <p>On which of the following does the activity of a radioactive source depend?</p>
-        <p>(1) the nature of the nuclear radiation emitted (2) the half-life of the source (3) the number of active nuclei</p>
+        <ol class="stem-list">

+          <li>the half-life of the nuclide</li>
+          <li>the number of undecayed nuclei</li>
+        </ol>
         <div class="choices">
           <button type="button" data-choice="A">(1) only</button>
           <button type="button" data-choice="B">(3) only</button>
@@ -198,32 +295,75 @@
           <button type="button" data-choice="D">(2) and (3) only</button>
         </div>
         <p class="feedback"></p>
+        <div class="explain" hidden>
+          <p>\( A = kN \): the number of nuclei (3) and, through \( k = \ln 2 / T_{1/2} \), the half-life (2). Whether the decay gives α, β or γ has no bearing on <em>how many</em> decays occur per second. Answer <b>D</b>.</p>
+        </div>
+      </div>
+      <div class="check" data-check="mc" data-answer="C">

+        <p>Two samples P and Q are of the same radionuclide. P has twice as many undecayed nuclei as Q. Which of the following is correct?</p>
+        <div class="choices">

+          <button type="button" data-choice="B">P and Q have the same activity because they are the same nuclide.</button>
+          <button type="button" data-choice="C">P has twice the activity of Q, and both activities halve in the same time.</button>
